Carsten Höller’s Spiraling Silver Slides Appear in Mexico City as a Permanent Installation

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Belgian artist Carsten Höller’s giant, spiraling silver slides have made their way to Arte Abierto Polanco in Mexico City as part of a permanent installation.

The large-scale installation, titled Mexico City Slides (2026), features four spiral slides integrated into Antara, an upscale open-air shopping mall that houses retail, dining, entertainment, offices and public space. Höller’s installation was commissioned by Arte Abierto and Sordo Madaleno Arquitectos and can be seen from Antara’s gardens, plazas and pedestrian promenade. Related Articles Carsten Höller Booed at New York's NFT-Themed Dreamverse Event Carsten Höller on His New Participatory Work: 'It Makes the Phone Freak Out'

The Mexico City Slides installation ranges from 39 to 125 feet in height and 102 to 295 feet in length. All four spiral slides are open to the public and allow four people to ride simultaneously from various entrances such as the family-style restaurants, the Google offices, the Rosewood Hotel, and the Arte Abierto Art Space. All four slides end at the same exit. The artist said he wanted the installation to be an “equalizer” and a “voluptuous panic upon an otherwise lucid mind” for participants and viewers. In 2015, Höller told The Guardian that it’s “impossible to travel down a slide without smiling.”

Höller’s installation is part of a work-in-progress franchise that he started in 1998. Different variations of the slides have appeared across the world, from Test Site (2006) at Tate Modern in London, Victoria Slide (2011) at the New Museum in New York, Vitra Slide Tower (2014) at the Vitra Campus in Weil am Rhein, Florence Experiment Slides (2018) at Palazzo Strozzi in Florence and DAC Slide (2020) at the Danish Architecture Centre in Copenhagen.

GdS: A fourth failed test – Amorim risks going off track with consistent rotations

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

After another poor performance in midweek, questions are being asked about Ruben Amorim’s AC Milan squad and his ability to rotate.

Amorim isn’t exactly to blame for last night; after a busy October, he had to rotate. However, results aren’t as strong when he opts to remove the starters and last night’s defeat to Ferencvaros is only the latest example of this issue.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

Gazzetta dello Sport writes that the Rossoneri are a team that loses its bearings whenever a rotation occurs. Indeed, even a few months in, Amorim can only trust a few of his players to work in his system.

The Derby della Madonnina should’ve fueled the squad, but a rotated squad lacked intensity and showed no signs of desire to build some momentum. Indeed, the gap between the starters and the reserves was stark, too stark.

Across the pitch, performances were poor – as the fan ratings showed – and age cannot be an excuse. After all, they were not hung out to dry against a European elite, it was a winnable game.

This fourth test, as Gazzetta puts it, was a failure and it’s now the third time that they’ve fallen in Europe. Even against Bologna, with slight rotations, they fell. So, Amorim may now be reluctant to rotate as heavily, but that brings up new issues. If they keep going at this rate, they’ll head off track.

Judge puts Trump's arch project on hold despite commission's approval

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The latest rendering of the controversial arch, complete with a winged Statue of Liberty holding a torch with real fire United States Triumphal Arch Foundation

Moments after the National Capital Planning Commission (NCPC)—a review panel stacked with Trump administration appointees—approved plans for a Triumphal Arch at Memorial Circle in Washington, DC, on Thursday night (5 November), a federal judge blocked construction on the project for 14 days.

The move obstructs the government’s stated aim of starting work on one of US president Donald Trump’s pet building projects immediately, despite lawsuits and public protests, and allows a court case brought by a Vietnam War veteran and the attorneys general of Washington, DC, and Virginia to continue.

In her 33-page decision, Judge Tanya S. Chutkan outlined the government’s shifting justifications for building the arch, which have included a desire to celebrate the 250th anniversary of the signing of the Declaration of Independence, a more than century-old congressional act to commemorate the end of the Civil War, and even supposed national security concerns. “These arguments are unavailing,” she wrote.

She also found that “harm is certain” to be inflicted on both Washington, DC, and Virginia if construction on the arch is allowed to go ahead, as it would interrupt traffic at a vital artery into the capital for years and result in unknown damage and expense.

Trump has pushed to fast-track the project, so that it could be mostly completed before he leaves office in 2028. His efforts to leave his mark on the capital include the bulldozing of the historic East Wing of the White House to make room for a 1,000-seat ballroom, a slapdash relining of the Reflecting Pool on the National Mall, and plans to paint the Eisenhower Executive Office Building white.

In another move to secure his design legacy, Trump signed an order on Thursday that aims to make the Oval Office—which he has decorated with gilded adornments—into a national monument. “It will not be allowed to be touched by anybody, destroyed, ripped down by stupid people in order to build something in a slightly different shape," Trump told reporters without a trace of irony. Presidents can create national monuments, based on the 1906 Antiquities Act, but this refers to federal lands set aside for preservation.

Trump’s grand plans have so far been helped by the fact that the committees overseeing preservation and design review for the capital are packed with appointees loyal to the president. This was most evident in Thursday’s meeting of the NCPC, where chairman Mark Paoletta—who also serves as general counsel of the Office of Management and Budget and deputy director of the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau—continually presented the government’s legal arguments in favour of building the arch.

When a smaller contingent of non-administration commissioners, led by DC city council chair Phil Mendelson, moved to delay the vote on the arch to December, vice chair Stuart Levenbach—who also works in the Office of Management and Budget as chief statistician—noted that "the project proponent is under a time constraint and wants the decision today".

No explanation was given for what that time constraint involved however. In the end, the NCPC voted along loyalist lines, with the eight administration-appointed members voting to approve the arch plans and four voting against.

The vote came at the end of more than eight hours of public comments from more than 100 speakers, including public officials, veterans and families of fallen soldiers—many of them calling in from an all-day protest taking place at Memorial Circle.

Most of their testimony was excoriating, and many urged the commission not to kowtow to the self-aggrandising whims of the president. Congressman Jared Huffman, the ranking Democrat on the House Natural Resources Committee, said the arch lacks congressional authorisation and goes against the Height of Buildings Act and other preservation laws. He warned that those working to move forward with the arch “will put themselves at legal risk".

"I urge all officials and firms involved with this illegal project to consider the legal hazards they may be facing by advancing it because, in case you hadn't heard, we'll soon have a new congress that takes the rule of law very seriously," said Huffman, referencing the outcome of the recent US midterm elections.

There are more than a dozen documented archaeological and historic sites within one mile of the proposed arch location, according to a new report, and additional undocumented materials may be present

In a National Capital Planning Commission meeting, one public commenter compared the project to the ending of ‘Death Becomes Her’

The order by a district court judge for Washington, DC, requires the White House to seek approval from Congress for Donald Trump’s $400m construction project

Meanwhile another giant memorial-arch project in Salt Lake City, Utah, has local residents up in arms

Where to Eat Gluten-Free in Montreal

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Recently I’ve noticed more and more food people going to Montreal—and who can blame them? This laid-back bilingual gem on the St. Lawrence River is one of North America’s very best food cities. Personally I’ve loved it since my first visit almost 20 years ago. Montreal’s wonderful food scene draws me back year after year, sometimes multiple times,and by now the six-hour drive from my home in New York City feels like a breeze. It helps to know that my top meals of the year are waiting just across the border.

But for the past two years, following my wife’s celiac diagnosis, I have been navigating the city a little differently. When I was younger, trips to Montreal meant poutine, bagels, smoked-meat sandwiches, and a plethora of other canonical Québécois favorites that happen to be full of gluten. These days we prioritize other local favorites and have developed a dependable roster of businesses we trust—and that don’t feel like a compromise. Luckily there are a ton of them; we’ve been thrilled to discover that Montreal is a paradise for gluten-free eating.

In my opinion the only gluten-free Montreal-style bagels worth sampling are located in Burlington, Vermont. But Montreal is a city that takes its baked goods seriously, and it’s home to a handful of excellent dedicated gluten-free bakeries. You will not miss the bagels. Instead how about a croissant?

Any gluten-free eater knows croissants are one of the hardest pastries to get right without gluten. As a rule my wife and I don’t really bother—unless we’re in Montreal. At Boulangerie Le Marquis, near Jean-Talon Market, and sister business Le Marquis Signature Santé, in Old Montreal, magnificent pains au chocolat and gluten-free croissants—available in plain, almond, and chocolate-almond varieties—arrive hot out of the oven all morning long. Flaky laminated pastry is a near-impossible feat in the gluten-free world, but Le Marquis has perfected it. We favor the pains au chocolat and almond croissants, but it’s tough to go wrong.

Even more unique is the gluten-free manouché at La Bolo à Lolo, a GF market and takeaway in the Plateau. Chef and owner Gordy Moufarrej emigrated from Lebanon to Montreal as a boy and uses his mother’s homemade za’atar in these addictive flatbreads. Made from Moufarrej’s proprietary GF flour blend, they are topped with a paste of olive oil and za’atar, plus cheese if you want (you do). A number of sandwiches made with the same base are available, but we’ve never gotten past the manouché—on our last visit to Montreal, we went three days in a row.

Perhaps unsurprisingly for a French-speaking city, crepes are another great option, and Montreal boasts an amazing spot for 100% buckwheat Breton-style galettes. At Crêperie du Marché, a bustling stall at Jean-Talon Market, cooks turn out a dedicated gluten-free menu of lacy galettes with both savory and sweet fillings. It’s a personal choice, but we like the cheeky selections that mix both: ham, cheese, eggs, potatoes, and a drizzle of maple syrup. Why not?

Nearby is cheery, quirky Cantine Panella, a dedicated gluten-free bakery and café run by the Peruvian chef Alessandra Perona. Her South American sensibility means breads are made with flours like quinoa, cassava, and sweet potato, which gives them a delightful chewiness. We like to stock up on a few of these to bring home, though they sometimes don’t last the drive.

Of all Montreal’s classic foods, rotisserie chicken might be my favorite. At Québécois-style rotisseries it’s slow-roasted on a spit and accompanied by gravy; at Portuguese spots it’s often spatchcocked and grilled over charcoal, then served with piri-piri sauce. In both cases gluten sometimes spoils the fun: Traditional establishments like the legendary Chalet Bar-B-Q and Portuguese eatery Ma Poule Mouillée both use wheat flour in their sauces. No matter—these are far from your only options.

Piri-Piri Chicken at Rôtisserie Coco Rico

At Rôtisserie Coco Rico, a Plateau institution open since 1970, the chicken is spit-roasted and served with GF piri-piri in a mélange of the two dominant local styles. On the side fries from a dedicated GF fryer are among the city’s best (and this is a city with great french fries), but don’t sleep on the roasted potatoes, full of savory chicken flavor.

More refined, and representing the French Canadian tradition, is Little Italy’s Rôtisserie La Lune, from the team behind überpopular restaurant and wine bar Mon Lapin. Gorgeous, plump birds are served with rich gluten-free gravy and excellent fries that come from a blessedly dedicated fryer. The same fryer is also responsible for the unbelievable Croqu’Ailes Farcies XL, which are deboned chicken wings stuffed with more chicken and deep-fried. Every meal here should start with these. But the kitchen has an equally deft hand with seasonal salads and veggie-focused sides—almost always gluten-free and an essential counterweight on the table.

Down the street from La Lune, Mon Lapin is equally adept at catering to gluten-free diners: On two recent visits most of the menu was in play. Dishes change with the seasons, but friendly servers will guide your choices (and help you navigate the impressive wine list). Make sure to save room for the restaurant’s signature buckwheat layer cake, accented with buckwheat honey caramel, fromage blanc, and crisp shards of buckwheat tuile. I’m not sure there’s a better gluten-free dessert on the continent.

Mon Lapin’s co-owners, chefs Jessica Noël and Marc-Olivier Frappier, and wine director Vanya Filipovic, are all alums of the trendsetting Little Burgundy eatery and wine bar Vin Papillon, whose chef and owner happens to be celiac. Best known as half of the duo that opened Montreal icon Joe Beef, Fred Morin now presides over those two restaurants, plus the rotisserie McKiernan and gastropub Liverpool House. His kitchens have airtight allergy protocols and are particularly sensitive to gluten-free diners because of Morin’s own celiac status. And because so many Montreal chefs have passed through Morin’s kitchens, his influence looms large over the city’s fine dining scene—and its spirit of hospitality toward those with allergies and intolerances.

While all of Morin’s restaurants are fantastic, Vin Papillon is the one we come back to every year—its Jambon de la Petite-Bourgogne is one of the world’s great meat-and-cheese plates, featuring house-cured ham, the Quebec sheep-milk cheese le Fleur des Monts, and a sneaky application of brown butter that brings together all the nutty, savory notes. For dessert the restaurant often serves a gluten-free carrot cake ice cream sandwich with caramel sauce in a silver goblet.

Another Joe Beef group alum, Ariel Schor, runs one of our favorite Verdun neighborhood spots with his brother, Pablo. Both natives of Argentina with Jewish roots, the Schors run a restaurant called Beba with a particular affinity for offal (organ meats) and a surprisingly light touch with creative veg dishes that change seasonally. Swiss chard leaves are stuffed with zucchini and chard stems and served with a sweet-and-sour condiment of onions, raisins, and pine nuts. Medjool dates are impishly filled with foie gras and topped with pistachio. Elsewhere on the menu expect things like tongue, rabbit, chorizo, and tripe—dishes that tend to be gluten-free.

When you’re craving Gallic comfort food, Montreal overdelivers. I used to think there were Leméac people and L’Express people, that one couldn’t possibly love Montreal’s two most prominent old-school bistros equally. I was wrong. The late local architect Luc Laporte designed both dining rooms, and they each have the buzzing, joyful energy most restaurants covet.

L’Express is my first love, a Montreal stalwart open since 1980 that I’ve been to more times than I can count. Its main virtues for gluten-free eaters are a dedicated fryer and incredibly good french fries—available as a stand-alone side or, even better, as part of steak frites. There are plenty of other GF mains and entrées, but room must be saved for the enormous île flottante, a classic French meringue-based dessert encased in a hardened caramel dome and surrounded by shards of candied almond. For something on the lighter side, try the unique crème brûlée with wild sweet clover from Quebec, sometimes called boreal vanilla.

Leméac does not have a dedicated fryer, so fries don’t work for celiacs. But I promise you won’t miss them. We favor the textbook duck confit, served with roasted potatoes and a bright green salad. For dessert there’s an unmissable gluten-free chocolate-and-banana moelleux, a decadent cake served with caramel and popcorn ice cream, along with many other classics, including crème caramel.

Montreal is also home to a number of fabulous Middle Eastern and North African restaurants, some of which can cater to gluten-free diners—albeit with asterisks. We love the standout Damas, a Syrian fine-dining spot on a pretty corner in Outremont, though gluten-free choices are limited. Still, it’s worth the trip for the ground lamb and eggplant kebab grilled over open charcoal and accompanied by charred pepper, tomato butter, and gobs of garlic labneh and the dreamy hummus topped with lamb shawarma and pine nuts, which can be served with crudités rather than pita. Both are among the best things I’ve eaten in recent memory.

Le Petit Alep, a neighborhood institution near Jean-Talon, offers more Syrian and Armenian options. The marvelous mezes are largely gluten-free and served—upon request—with crudités; savory, comforting mujaddara is made with lentils and rice, no bulgur, barley, or other glutenous grains. End with the house herbal tea, a beautiful floral mixture of cardamom pods, mint, rosewater, and orange blossom. I’ve tried to make it at home, but I can’t quite replicate it. That’s okay—I’ll be back next year.

Apple Says There Are Now 100,000 Apps Built for the iPhone Duo

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Our gifting experts are obsessively searching for holiday gifts that are unique, thoughtful and suitable for any budget.

Our expert, award-winning staff selects the products we cover and rigorously researches and tests our top picks. If you buy through our links, we may earn a commission.

The new 4:3 inner screen doesn't play nicely with every app, but here's some that work so far.

Apple’s first foldable, the iPhone Duo, may have come last to the foldable party, as CNET’s review noted, but it’s certainly not least. Senior reporter Abrar Al-Heeti praised it for the iOS 27 integration across both displays and its impressive durability, which has crossed 300,000 folds in our test and counting.

However, as the Duo has filtered down to the general public, some new owners have noted that some of their favorite apps aren’t well optimized for the larger 4:3 inner display. I noticed this myself when I opened the iPhone Duo and launched Discord, finding the app centered on the screen with black space on both sides, rather than expanding to fill the panel.

That’s not unusual for a phone with a brand-new hardware design, and we’ve seen it on many Android foldables, with optimized apps taking a little bit of time to roll out. Apple has confirmed that this is already changing, with over 100,000 apps built for the iPhone Duo to more fully take advantage of the 4:3 main screen.

“Today, there are well over 100,000 apps built for iPhone Duo already live on the App Store, and thousands of optimized apps are being added to the store each day,” said Susan Prescott, Apple’s vice president of Worldwide Developer Relations in a comment sent to CNET.

“In the last month alone, thousands of developers have joined us for workshops in 19 cities around the world to experience iPhone Duo firsthand and bring updated apps to their users,” Prescott said. “The enthusiasm has been incredible, and we’re excited to see the pace of updates continue to accelerate as more and more apps optimize for iPhone Duo.”

Among the apps, Microsoft Word and Outlook have been adjusted to look similar to their desktop versions, while Zoom uses the inner display as a bigger video conference room. Additionally, the company has confirmed that sports fans can use the NBA and NHL apps to follow live games and highlights on the larger screen.

Etsy and LTK Creator will also show more products in the feed, and productivity apps like Goodnotes and Extra will make more use of the additional screen real estate. There’s also Halide Mark III, a pro camera app that adapts to every pose, from a viewfinder on the cover screen when closed to a waist-level viewfinder with one tap when half open.

The iOS 27.2 update should also add support for Apple Pencil (USB-C), letting Duo users in various compatible apps, like Apple Notes, work directly on documents.

Apple shared a list of apps that have been recently optimized for the iPhone Duo, breaking them down into categories like Work, AI, Finances, News and more.

This isn’t an exclusive list by any means. CNET previously confirmed seven iPhone Duo-ready apps that make the most of Apple’s foldable screen: Netflix, NYT Cooking, Fantastical, Edits by Instagram, TikTok, Instagram and Toastie.

Apple fans have put together their own “does it work on iPhone Duo?” web tracker, giving you a sense of whether an app is fully optimized, partially fits or is still awaiting updates. I can confirm that Discord is currently listed as “partially fits,” meaning it uses most of the inner screen but not all of it, which tracks with my own experience with the letterboxing.

We expect that this is only the beginning, and we’ll see the list above expand in the coming weeks as updated apps are submitted to the App Store. Going forward, you should also see spotlights for iPhone Duo apps in categories like “Great Apps and Games” and “Popular Apps.” Apple is making it easier to identify apps compatible with the iPhone Duo by adding a Made for iPhone Duo badge to product pages.

Trek’s New Domane Trades IsoSpeed for Wider Tires. I Think It Works

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

After riding the $11,499 SLR, I think the $4,200 SL 6 AXS is the smartest buy.

Gear-obsessed editors choose every product we review. We may earn commission if you buy from a link. How we test gear.

This Review Continues for MembersMy full test and verdict are here. Next, I want to hear what you think. Bicycling members can continue the discussion with me on Discord, where I’ll answer questions and hear how the Domane works for riders beyond my test. The invitation appears after the review.

If you want a comfortable, relatively upright road bike for rough pavement, long rides, and some light gravel, the new Domane belongs on your list. It feels more eager under power than previous versions, while keeping the stability and confidence that have always made the bike good at everyday riding.

The version I’d point most people toward is the $4,200 SL 6 AXS. It gets the same geometry, tire clearance, and basic frame features as the expensive SLR models, along with electronic shifting and carbon wheels. The SLR saves weight and opens the door to Trek’s Project One customization. Those are real advantages, but they don’t make it a fundamentally different bike.

I tested the $11,499 SLR 9 AXS 2x. It weighed 16.3 pounds in size M. The bike I’d recommend first costs less than half as much.

BIKE TESTED: Trek Gen. 5 Domane SLR 9 AXS 2xACTUAL WEIGHT 16.3 pounds (Size M, as tested) USE Road, light gravel PRICE AS TESTED $11,499 MODEL PRICE RANGE $2,400 to $11,499; $14,999 limited edition ICON No. 76FRAME ONLY OPTION: Yes. $3,700 (SLR) $2,000 (SL)CUSTOMIZABLE BUILDS Yes, SLR models through Project One SIZES OFFERED XS, S, M, ML, L, XL WEIGHT LIMIT 125 kg/275 lb (bicycle, rider, and cargo) TIRE CLEARANCE 40mm BB STANDARD T47 threaded BRAKE HOSE ROUTING Internal, RCS headset; under bar/stem on most buildsDRIVETRAIN COMPATIBILITY 1x and 2x, electronic shifting only DERAILLEUR HANGER UDH WARRANTY Lifetime, frame and rigid fork, original owner CRASH REPLACEMENT Yes, Trek Carbon Care discounted replacement

I’ve ridden every generation of the Domane, including several of the more aggressive RSL versions. Over the years, Trek added adjustable IsoSpeed, front IsoSpeed, internal storage, aero shaping, and hidden cables. The fifth-generation bike pulls back on much of that.

The Gen 5 feels more solid and reactive underfoot than its predecessors. It’s stiffer when I get on the pedals, accelerates with more snap, and climbs with a crispness that used to elude the Domane. In that way, it feels more like a Madone than any previous version.

It hasn’t lost the stability and confidence that make the Domane a good everyday road bike. I can push it hard down a descent without feeling like I’m taking a test of nerves. The fit is excellent, too. The taller front end should help more riders find a position that’s comfortable and aerodynamic.

And no, it doesn’t feel slow on 35mm tires. The bike feels quick and responsive. The wider tires are part of what makes it work, not an anchor holding it back.

The original Domane’s signature feature was IsoSpeed, a mechanism that let the seat tube flex to add comfort. Trek developed it over several generations, then removed it from the bike that introduced it.

The new Domane gets much of its comfort from wider tires at lower pressures, along with frame and seatpost tuning. Most models come with 35mm tires, and the frame clears up to 40mm. Trek says that combination provides enough comfort that IsoSpeed’s remaining benefit isn’t worth its weight and complexity.

IsoSpeed still works. Trek told me that with the same 35mm tires, the Gen 4 transmits slightly less vibrational energy than the Gen 5. Trek also says the Gen 5 on 35mm tires comes within 5 percent of the Gen 4 on its stock 32mm tires at the rear. At the front, Trek says the two generations perform similarly.

Those are Trek’s test results, not measurements I made. But the decision makes sense to me. Wider tires add traction and capability that IsoSpeed couldn’t provide. Trek decided the system’s remaining comfort advantage wasn’t enough to justify keeping it.

If you own an older Domane, what did IsoSpeed do for you? Did you notice a difference when you moved to wider tires, or do you miss the old system? I’d like to hear what you’ve felt on real roads.

According to Trek’s white paper, the Gen 5 frame is about 292 grams lighter than Gen 4. It also eliminates IsoSpeed and its associated maintenance.

The frame uses a threaded T47 bottom bracket, a round fork steerer, a Universal Derailleur Hanger, and a standard 27.2mm seatpost. For a modern performance bike, there’s not much proprietary weirdness here. That’s a feature.

The internal storage compartment is gone, too. I’ll miss it. It was useful. Trek says its research showed that many Domane owners weren’t using it. The new frame retains fender mounts, top-tube mounts, and frame-bag mounts. I like the trade: less hardware and complexity, with straightforward ways to carry gear when you need to.

The cable routing is another smart compromise. On most models, brake hoses run beneath the two-piece bar and stem before entering the frame at the headset. It looks clean, but changing a bar or stem doesn’t become a major repair job. Fit changes and service matter more to me than hiding every cable at any cost.

The SL and SLR share the same geometry, tire clearance, seatpost size, bottom bracket, UDH, and general feature set. The SL uses Trek’s 500 Series carbon; the SLR uses lighter 900 Series carbon. Trek says both frames are designed to the same stiffness requirements. The SL supports mechanical drivetrains; the SLR is electronic-shifting only.

The SLR primarily buys lower weight, access to Trek’s highest-end builds, and the ability to customize components through Project One. Buyers can specify details such as crank length, handlebar width, and stem length. If you’re spending this much on a bike, getting those dimensions right from the factory is a meaningful advantage.

But the SLR doesn’t change the core experience enough to be the obvious choice for most riders. I’d look closely at the $4,200 SL 6 AXS first. It has a carbon frame, electronic shifting, carbon wheels, 40mm tire clearance, and the same accessible fit. The carbon range starts at $2,400 with the Tiagra SL 4. The SLR 9 AXS 2x I tested costs $11,499.

Trek also offers an ultralight SLR 9 AXS 1x with 28mm tires. Trek describes it as the racier interpretation of the Domane, but it did not vibration-test that configuration. If comfort is a big reason you’re considering the bike, pay attention to the tire setup, not just the frame name.

If you’re shopping for a Domane, which versions are you weighing? Would you spend more for the SLR’s lower weight and Project One options, or put that money toward another bike or gear?

Trek says it applied aero lessons from the Gen 8 Madone, but aerodynamics weren’t a major priority. In a pedaling-mannequin test, Trek claims the Gen 5 improved by 0.6 watts over Gen 4.

That seems appropriate. Trek prioritized weight, simplicity, and ride quality instead of chasing another aero headline.

The fifth-generation Domane is an excellent road bike. It’s light, comfortable, stable, and lively. It’s easier to adjust and service than many integrated performance bikes, retains useful mounts, and drops features that added weight and complexity without improving the bike enough to justify them.

The old Domanes were more technically adventurous. I miss that part of the story more than I miss riding them.

For most riders, I think Gen 5 is the right Trek road bike. The SL 6 AXS is the model I’d point people toward first. The SLR makes sense if you want the lighter frame and factory customization, but the expensive build doesn’t change the core experience. Taken as a whole, the new Domane is my favorite drop-bar bike Trek makes.

That’s my take after riding the bike. Now I want to hear from people who own a Domane, have ridden an earlier version, or are considering the Gen 5. What questions do you still have? What would you like me to investigate or compare? I’ll continue the conversation on Discord and bring the most useful questions and experiences back here.

What do you think of the new Trek Domane? What questions do you still have? What did I screw up? If you own or have ridden the Gen 5 Domane, share your impressions.

Bicycling members can join me in The Test Ride on Discord to continue this review. The invitation is below.

Find the Trek Gen 5 Domane post in the bike-reviews forum. Tell me what you ride and what you’re deciding between. Your feedback and insights will help me write better reviews.

A gear editor for his entire career, Matt’s journey to becoming a leading cycling tech journalist started in 1995, and he’s been at it ever since; likely riding more cycling equipment than anyone on the planet along the way. Previous to his time with Bicycling, Matt worked in bike shops as a service manager, mechanic, and sales person. Based in Durango, Colorado, he enjoys riding and testing any and all kinds of bikes, so you’re just as likely to see him on a road bike dressed in Lycra at a Tuesday night worlds ride as you are to find him dressed in a full face helmet and pads riding a bike park on an enduro bike. Official | Paul Nebel signs contract extension amid Mainz 05 form crisis

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Bundesliga club Mainz 05 have officially confirmed that attacking midfielder Paul Nebel has signed a contract extension. The 24-year-old’s previous agreement had been scheduled to expire in June 2027, but the Rheinhessen have now secured the services of their long-serving academy graduate beyond the current season.

Mainz announced the agreement on Friday without disclosing the duration of Nebel’s new contract. The decision represents an important commitment to a player who has spent more than a decade at the club, having originally joined Mainz’s youth academy from Kickers Offenbach in 2016.

Nebel made his professional debut in 2020 but initially struggled to establish himself in the Bundesliga. A productive two-year loan spell at Karlsruher SC between 2022 and 2024 subsequently helped accelerate his development, allowing the versatile attacker to return to his parent club as a considerably more experienced professional.

The former Germany U21 international subsequently emerged as an important figure in Mainz’s attacking setup, contributing significantly to the club’s impressive 2024/25 Bundesliga campaign and earning a senior Germany call-up in September 2025.

His development has nevertheless been far from straightforward.

The contract extension follows a challenging period during which Nebel’s long-term future in Mainz had appeared uncertain. The attacking midfielder endured a disappointing 2025/26 Bundesliga campaign after initially struggling to reproduce the performances that established him as an important player under former head coach Bo Henriksen.

His fortunes improved considerably during the spring. Following an injury to Nadiem Amiri, Nebel began to reestablish himself in Mainz’s attacking hierarchy. The youngster eventually produced a remarkable sequence of performances, including a brace in March’s Bundesliga victory over Eintracht Frankfurt. As documented in Get German Football News‘ coverage of Nebel’s resurgence, Fischer and captain Silvan Widmer praised the midfielder for overcoming his earlier difficulties through perseverance and hard work.

The renewed momentum nevertheless suffered another interruption during the summer. Nebel sustained an adductor injury during Mainz’s August training camp, ruling him out of the opening stages of the new campaign. The setback arrived at an unfortunate time. As GGFN‘s extensive Mainz preseason tactical preview had already suggested, competition for attacking positions under Fischer was becoming increasingly intense.

Nebel has only recently returned to competitive football, making substitute appearances against Schalke and Werder Bremen before earning his first Bundesliga start of the season against SV Elversberg. The latter encounter proved deeply disappointing. Nebel struggled to influence Mainz’s comprehensive 4-1 defeat and was substituted in the 63rd minute, receiving a 4/10 rating from GGFN.

His difficulties, however, have coincided with a much broader deterioration in Mainz’s performances.

Despite Friday’s positive contract announcement, Mainz enter Saturday’s Bundesliga encounter with Bayern Munich facing considerable questions about their recent form. Fischer’s side opened the campaign encouragingly, collecting ten points from their first six Bundesliga fixtures and briefly occupying fifth place.

Three consecutive competitive defeats have since dramatically altered the atmosphere around the club. Mainz first surrendered a home fixture against Werder Bremen, losing 2-3 before suffering a 0-2 DFB-Pokal elimination against second-division VfL Bochum. Last Friday’s humiliating defeat against promoted Elversberg subsequently brought their problems into sharper focus.

The difficulties extend beyond the results themselves.

Mainz currently rank 15th in the Bundesliga for total distance covered, 17th for sprints, and last in the league for intensive runs. Those figures are particularly concerning for a club traditionally associated with physical commitment, relentless pressing, and aggressive defensive work.

The decline has prompted questions about whether Mainz’s players have struggled to maintain the intensity required following an encouraging start to the campaign. Fischer acknowledged the negative trend during Friday’s pre-match press conference, emphasizing that his players must collectively return to the fundamental principles of Mainz football.

The Swiss trainer admitted that his proposed solutions were hardly revolutionary. Running, tackling, intensity, and aggression remain the essential ingredients. “I’m just boring, and I keep repeating myself,” Fischer joked. “You’re not getting anything new.”

Fischer must also contend with significant personnel shortages. Dominik Kohr and Silas remain unavailable through injury, while Phillipp Mwene and Stefan Posch are suspended. Mainz’s decision to extend Nebel’s contract therefore represents an important element of stability during an increasingly difficult period.

The youngster has already demonstrated that he can recover from setbacks and become an influential Bundesliga performer. His resurgence during the previous campaign provided one of the more encouraging individual stories of Fischer’s tenure.

Now both player and club must attempt to rediscover that momentum.

Mainz sporting director Niko Bungert praised the midfielder’s development and emphasized his importance to the club’s long-term plans.

“Paul is a prime example of how a player from our own youth academy can develop into a very important player for our Bundesliga team through passion, patience, and a down-to-earth attitude,” Bungert explained. “Paul is an important and reliable mainstay of our team and someone who has shown in recent years that he is always there when we need him. We’re delighted that we’ve found a very good solution for our future together.”

Nebel himself expressed his satisfaction with the agreement and reaffirmed his commitment to the club.

“Mainz 05 is my footballing home,” Nebel said. “I grew up at this club, spent formative years in the youth academy here, and developed into a Bundesliga professional. I’m very happy that I have the confidence of the people responsible and that we can continue on this path together. I’m highly motivated to repay this trust with performances and give everything for the club and the fans. We still have a great deal we want to achieve together as a team this season.”

Justices consider when a constitutional rule is “previously unavailable” to prisoner

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

The Supreme Court on Wednesday heard argument in Guerrero v. Johnson, a case about whether a Texas man on death row can bring a second federal post-conviction challenge to his sentence by arguing that, under updated psychiatric standards, he was found intellectually disabled and therefore cannot be executed.

Except in a few narrow circumstances, the Antiterrorism and Effective Death Penalty Act of 1996 bars prisoners from filing a petition for federal post-conviction relief challenging the grounds for their detention when an earlier petition has already been rejected. One of these exceptions is when such a claim relies on “a new rule of constitutional law, made retroactive to cases on collateral review by the Supreme Court, that was previously unavailable.” The other is if facts “underlying the [initial] claim” would show “by clear and convincing evidence that, but for constitutional error, no reasonable fact finder would have found the applicant guilty of the underlying offense.”

In 2007, Dexter Johnson was convicted of capital murder and sentenced to death for his role in the double murder of 23-year-old Maria Aparece and 17-year-old Huy Ngo. Five years before his conviction, the Supreme Court held in Atkins v. Virginia that executing people with intellectual disabilities violates the Eighth Amendment. A defense expert at Johnson’s trial concluded that, under the diagnostic standards employed at the time, Johnson was not intellectually disabled. Following his conviction, Johnson filed an initial habeas petition and invoked Atkins to argue – unsuccessfully – that its protections should extend to people with mental illness.

In May 2013, while Johnson’s first federal petition was pending, the American Psychiatric Association published the DSM-5, a new edition of its diagnostic manual that deemphasized IQ cutoffs. Texas’ highest criminal court adopted this in 2018. In 2019, Johnson scored 70 on an IQ test, and an expert concluded he met the DSM-5’s criteria for intellectual disability. Johnson then filed a successive habeas challenge in the district court claiming that he could not be executed under Atkins. The trial court subsequently ruled that his claim (based on the later diagnostic criteria) had been “previously unavailable,” and the U.S. Court of Appeals for the 5th Circuit affirmed that decision. The 5th Circuit denied rehearing, and Texas came to the Supreme Court, which agreed to hear the case in June.

At oral argument, Texas Solicitor General William Peterson told the justices that Congress “chose to allow new claims in these applications only in limited circumstances that do not include changes in the diagnostic standards for intellectual disability.” There is no “new rule” “previously unavailable” here, because Atkins was decided before Johnson “even committed his crimes,” Peterson said.

Justice Elena Kagan pressed Peterson on why the DSM “isn’t … part of the law?” She suggested that the DSM-5 effectively created “a kind of Atkins I and an Atkins II” – first, a rule barring the execution of people with intellectual disabilities, followed by a “substantive standard” defining what it means to be intellectually disabled. Kagan later described the Atkins standard as akin to a rule with a “big gap in it” where “the DSM comes and plugs it in and it becomes part of the legal standard.”

The justices further debated what it means for a rule to be “available.” Kagan suggested, based on prior precedent, that something is “not available to you” if it makes you “run into a brick wall.” “[I]f I was going to be [previously] laughed out of court,” she said, “then, really, the rule was unavailable to me.”

Justice Ketanji Brown Jackson told Peterson that, as she understood AEDPA, Congress’ “policy choice was to give individuals one clear shot at bringing their constitutional claims.” Under Texas’ interpretation, she said, “Mr. Johnson will never get that opportunity,” which “seems like a really incongruous consequence.”

Justice Sonia Sotomayor, for her part, suggested that the outcome should turn on something like ripeness (whether a case is sufficiently developed for a court to decide). As she remarked to Peterson, “[i]f what we have said under Atkins is you cannot execute someone who is mentally ill, we have a certain way of determining that, and it doesn't become ripe until the DM-5 makes it ripe.”

Justice Brett Kavanaugh asked Peterson whether the court “could resolve this case solely on the new rule issue without addressing ‘previously unavailable.’” Peterson said it could, but that Texas was asking the court to decide the “previously unavailable” question.

On behalf of Johnson, Jeremy Schepers told the justices that “existence and availability aren’t the same thing.” Atkins existed, but Johnson’s claim – based on the change in diagnostic criteria – was not previously available. When the clinical standards for intellectual disability change, Schepers argued, “the Eighth Amendment’s protected class potentially changes as well. And in those rare circumstances, that can render a rule legally available for the first time.”

Justice Clarence Thomas was skeptical, asking, “Does Atkins ever stop being a new rule?” Schepers responded that Atkins “remains new now.” “What about Marbury v. Madison?” Thomas responded. Justice Amy Coney Barrett noted that when “Alexander Graham Bell invented the telephone, that was new.” But “I don't think anybody would describe the telephone as new today.”

Kavanaugh pointed Schepers to a “friend of the court” brief filed by Louisiana and 21 other states, which argued that because the medical standards are “constantly evolving,” Johnson’s position is “a recipe for eternal Atkins claims and zero finality.” Schepers said that was incorrect, because a state prisoner gets “one opportunity to raise any claim,” and “so a prisoner could not raise a second Atkins claim.”

At one point, Justice Samuel Alito asked about a future edition of the DSM that “makes smaller changes” that “provide a greater opportunity for success.” Schepers said that claim would fail under the 5th Circuit’s reading, because “[i]t’s only a question of whether the claim had a possibility of merit before.”

Emily Ferguson, an assistant to the U.S. solicitor general who argued as a “friend of the court” in support of Texas, emphasized that it is “difficult to imagine Congress enacting a law that would allow the American Psychiatric Association, without independent action of this Court, to create a new rule of constitutional law.” She also noted that the court had made clear “states would be responsible for the method of determining intellectual disability,” and it would then make little “sense for there to be an available successive application anytime the relevant state had a new … supreme court case … or even a state lower court case that changed the framework that the state was using.”

Alito asked Ferguson whether the “Atkins rule” was “that a person may not be executed if the person has an intellectual disability that is regarded as such by medical professionals,” or if “it is that a person may not be executed if the person has an intellectual disability that can't be justified by the recognized purposes of punishment,” for which “the DSM would be evidence but not part of the standard itself?” Ferguson responded that the “latter formulation captures what … the Atkins Eighth Amendment rule was.”

Perhaps the most noticeable takeaway from oral argument was how subdued the justices were. The chief justice did not ask any questions, and no justice appeared to take up Johnson’s request in his brief that the court dismiss the case as improvidently granted (that is, decide that it was a mistake to have agreed to hear the case in the first place and not resolve it), as the court did last term in Hamm v. Smith, another Atkins case.

A decision is expected by late June but is likely to come earlier.

Nora is an editorial assistant at SCOTUSblog, based in Washington, D.C.

Zach is the executive editor of SCOTUSblog.

Tech Moves: Fred Hutch leader goes to AWS; Temporal, Augmodo and Neuralwatt name execs

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Tech Moves covers notable hires, promotions and personnel changes in the Pacific NW tech community. Submissions: [email protected]

by Lisa Stiffler on Nov 6, 2026 at 10:06 amNovember 6, 2026 at 10:45 am

— Amitabha “Guppy” Gupta, a Fred Hutch Cancer Center associate director who helps researchers translate their work to broader audiences, is leaving after 14 years to join Amazon. Starting Nov. 16, he will be a strategic advisor for nonprofit research and advanced AI with AWS’ Global Nonprofit Business.

Gupta said technology was often an obstacle to scientific breakthroughs at Fred Hutch.

“It’s the infrastructure, data, and tools standing between a scientist and an answer,” he wrote on LinkedIn. In his new role, “I’ll work with researchers and research institutions on using cloud, high-performance computing, and AI to remove those barriers.”

— Microsoft board member ‌Emma Walmsley has been appointed CEO of HP, effective Jan. 4. Walmsley, who led the pharmaceutical giant GSK until December 2025, succeeds Bruce Broussard. Reuters notes that her seven years serving on the Microsoft board “are her closest brush with the tech sector” prior to taking the top job at the PC maker.

— Bas Nijholt, senior staff engineer at IonQ‘s Seattle-area facility, has resigned after three years to join an AI startup that’s still in stealth mode. At IonQ, the theoretical quantum mechanics expert built MindRoom, an open-source collaboration and knowledge-sharing platform that he calls “a company brain.”

“With this new opportunity, I get to keep building and turbocharging MindRoom with a team of people who are just as excited about AI as I am,” Nijholt said, adding that the tool will remain freely available.

Before IonQ, Nijholt spent nearly four years at Microsoft.

— Bellevue, Wash., startup Temporal, whose software keeps AI agents running reliably, has named Sean Heneghan as vice president of sales for the Americas. The company ranks No. 1 on the GeekWire 200, a list of the top Pacific Northwest startups. In September it raised $550 million, putting its valuation at $12.55 billion.

Temporal is fully remote, and Heneghan is based in the Chicago area. He joins the company from MongoDB.

— Doug Heimburger, a 26-year Amazon veteran, has changed roles, shifting from chief financial officer and vice president of Worldwide Operations to CFO/VP of Worldwide Stores. In the new position, Heimburger said he’ll help oversee efforts to improve delivery speed, reduce costs and increase growth of the Stores business. His first role at the company was data mining analyst.

— Seattle startup Augmodo has named Capri Brixey as head of retail sales. The company, which sells AI-powered wearable devices with cameras, raised $21 million in July to expand from in-store applications that let workers passively track inventory into automotive, warehouse and hospital settings. Augmodo ranks No. 122 on the GeekWire 200.

Ross Finman, founder and CEO of Augmodo, called Brixey “the right leader to accelerate our expansion” given her experience selling to both retail and warehouse operations. Her past roles include leadership positions at Coca-Cola and Kroger.

— Meaghan Davison Praznik was named vice president of marketing for Neuralwatt, a Seattle-based startup that built a tool for calculating, in real time, the carbon emissions of individual AI requests. Her former employers include Clif Bar, AllTrails and Merino Energy.

Because the carbon intensity of grid power varies throughout the day and across regions, Neuralwatt’s technology can help customers run compute-intensive operations when clean energy is more abundant.

— BuyWander, an auction-based online marketplace for returned and overstocked retail items, has named Chris Holm as senior director of product. Holm spent nine years at Amazon, leaving in 2024 after managing last-mile and same-day delivery technology and working on Amazon Fresh delivery. He has since served as a consultant for carbon removal startup Lithos Carbon and as a founder-in-residence at the startup studio Foundations.

In August, BuyWander moved its headquarters across Washington from Spokane to Kent. The company announced $21 million in new funding in September.

— T-Mobile’s Kevin Lau has joined the advisory board at the University of Washington’s College of Engineering. Lau has been with the telecom giant for 22 years over two stints and serves as VP of digital experience engineering. He holds bachelor’s, master’s and doctoral degrees in electrical engineering from the UW.

— Christian Smith, an Issaquah, Wash.-based tech leader, has been named president of revenue for Cloudflare, which provides security and speed services for websites and online applications. He will oversee worldwide go-to-market operations. Smith previously served as chief revenue officer for Splunk and Nintex.

Newborns Are Experiencing Life-Threatening Bleeding As More U.S. Parents Decline Vitamin K Shot, Studies Find

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Researchers say rising vaccine hesitancy is to blame.

Two new studies published this week highlight a sharp increase in life-threatening bleeding incidents in American newborns, which coincide with a rise in parental refusals of vitamin K shots at birth. Vitamin K is an essential component that allows blood to clot; babies are born deficient. The American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP) has recommended a vitamin K shot be administered at birth since 1961 in order to prevent severe internal bleeding, which can result in brain bleeding and even death up to six months after birth.

The studies both address the correlation between a rise in parents refusing vitamin K shots and severe bleeding incidents. One study assessed 5.2 million infants; the number of babies who didn’t receive the shot jumped from 3.1% in 2017 to 6.8% in 2025. Those numbers align with an uptick in bleeding events among American babies — between 2017 and 2025, the incidence of bleeding by 6 months increased from 225 to nearly 400 per 10,000 births.

The second study found that between 2017 and 2025, “the percentage of newborns who received injectable vitamin K decreased from 97.7% to 95%.” The rate of vitamin K deficiency (VKD) and vitamin K deficiency bleeding (VKDB) increased by 215% and 347% respectively. Only 19% of infants diagnosed VKDB had received their boost of vitamin K at birth.

Both study’s authors point to waning trust in public health following the COVID-19 pandemic, and the rise anti-vax sentiment in recent years thanks to HHS secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.’s crusade against the childhood vaccine schedule.

“Increases in VKD and VKDB — peaking in 2025 — occur against the backdrop of eroding trust in public health in the wake of the COVID-19 pandemic, increasing vaccine hesitancy, and growing parental unease about interventions in newborns. As with childhood immunizations, vitamin K prophylaxis may be a victim of its own success; the rarity of VKDB may leave today’s families largely unaware of its grave consequences,” wrote study authors.

Parents who declined vitamin K shots for their babies cited concerns about outcomes or effectiveness, toxicity of the ingredients, risk of cancer, and a desire for “natural” alternatives, researchers also found. In the 1990s, one study sparked concern there may be a link between vitamin K shots and an increased risk of childhood cancer. Since then, multiple studies have been done to investigate the potential link, and there was found to be no association between the two.

According to the AAP, the vitamin K shot has been proven to be safe and effective; it has been recommended for 65 years now. Vitamin K is a naturally occurring nutrient made in our intestines, and found in many types of food. It doesn’t cross the placenta well, so babies are born deficient and without the gut bacteria to produce their own. Without a vitamin K shot, babies are 81 times more likely to develop VKDB. For every five babies who develop VKDB, one will die.

A vitamin K shot is not a vaccine, but headlines around recommended childhood shots have sown mass confusion over the past year. In March, a federal judge determined that Health & Human Services (HHS) secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. violated federal law by changing the childhood vaccine schedule in January and dismissing long-standing members of the CDC’s Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices. His ruling resulted from a lawsuit filed by the American Academy of Pediatrics, the Infectious Diseases Society of America, and the Society for Maternal-Fetal Medicine. The judge called Kennedy’s actions “dangerous,” saying they “have sowed chaos and confusion for parents and pediatricians across the country.”

It’s likely pediatric vaccines will remain a hot political topic heading into 2027. On the heels of midterm elections, the AAP issued a statement congratulating a number of newly elected and re-elected pediatricians, and urging Congress to make evidence-based children’s health policies a priority.

US air traffic control enters the 21st century, starts to abandon floppy disks

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The FAA will need to come up with more money to finish the wider overhaul, though

The Federal Aviation Administration still uses computers that require floppy disks. But those are finally on the way out within the next few years.

Yes, it’s still 2026, and yes, you read that right: The FAA – the agency responsible for overseeing aviation safety in the US – is finally getting rid of four systems dating back to the 1980s that remain in use at more than 400 facilities around the country, the agency announced on Thursday.

“Good news America: floppy disks are out, 21st century tech is in,” Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said in the FAA’s announcement. Only a few decades late, Sean, but we’re sure the flying public appreciates it.

The new system, known as Enterprise Information Display System (E-IDS), is designed to consolidate data from various systems into a common digital platform, allowing air traffic controllers to access operational information and alerts needed to manage flights.

E-IDS is replacing four legacy systems: Information Display System 4, used to track weather and operational info; the Automated Surface Observing System Controller Equipment Information Display System, which also relays weather information; the NAS Information Display System, which relays information about airports and other airspace conditions; and the En Route Information Display System (ERIDS), which handles information about flights.

The FAA said E-IDS will improve how Pilot Reports and Notices to Airmen (NOTAMs) are collected and distributed. The agency has had its share of air traffic control SNAFUs, including one in 2023 that temporarily halted domestic flight departures nationwide after someone accidentally deleted a file from the NOTAM system.

The FAA said it has already lined up five facilities to receive E-IDS upgrades: airports in Atlantic City, Nashville, and Rochester, New York, along with data processing hubs in Atlanta and Salt Lake City. The agency plans to install the system at 435 sites by the end of 2028.

That 2028 deadline echoes the ambitious three-year timetable Duffy laid out last year for overhauling the nation's air traffic control system.

Aside from the four systems E-IDS will replace, the FAA has a lot of outdated technology, with the Government Accountability Office saying in 2024 that 51 of the agency’s 138 ATC systems were unsustainable due to lack of parts and funding to sustain or replace them.

Trump’s Big Beautiful Bill allocated $12.5 billion for the FAA’s overhaul efforts, but that’s far short of what’s actually needed. In December of last year, the FAA said that it needed an additional $20 billion to handle upgrade costs, and that appears to still be the case.

"To continue modernizing our air traffic control facility, Secretary Duffy and FAA Administrator Bedford are calling on Congress to pass the Modern Skies Act," an FAA spokesperson told The Register in an email. That bill is asking for $30 billion to get ATC modernization across the finish line, the spokesperson added.

"It will modernize our air traffic control system, replace crumbling aviation infrastructure, and build world-class airports across the country," they said. In other words, the FAA wants to get rid of floppy disks, but it's probably not accurate to say they're "out," as Duffy put it.

The GAO said in September that the FAA was still well short of its financial needs, with an additional $10.2 billion needed for the second phase of upgrades, which has yet to receive a completion date.

As with so many government ambitions, budgets often get in the way, and this wider modernization effort still seems more ambitious on paper than the FAA may be able to deliver. ®

Elliptic says Binance hackers swapped stolen tokens for $124M in bitcoin, exchange plans more withdrawal reopenings

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The laundering operation spans 20,000 wallets and 16 blockchains, but Elliptic says little has likely been cashed out.

The attackers in last week's $6.43 billion breach of the Binance crypto exchange have managed to swap around $124 million in altcoins into bitcoin, according to an update from blockchain analysis firm Elliptic.

This is on top of the $1.48 billion in bitcoin stolen in the attack, which is being attributed to North Korea.

Elliptic added that the laundering operation has thus far used more than 20,000 wallets across 16 blockchains and 65,000 transactions, in an attempt to obfuscate the source of the funds. Despite this, the firm estimated that only a "negligible amount" has likely been cashed out.

The largest portion of the converted bitcoin came from stolen XRP swapped through THORChain. Elliptic's report said that by early Friday, roughly $50 million in XRP had been swapped, primarily into bitcoin, with about $226 million remaining.

The report added that nearly all of the stolen Solana (SOL) has already been mixed, swapped, or bridged into bitcoin.

Around $3.11 billion, mostly in ether and bitcoin, remains in addresses linked to the attack, while $2.85 billion of the total  $6.43 billion has been frozen. It's unclear if the $470 million not accounted for here is due to price changes since the attack, is from funds that have already been cashed out, or is a mix of both.

The Block has reached out to the report's author, Tom Robinson, for clarification.

Elliptic wrote that it expects the attackers to swap the rest of the ether for bitcoin in small batches before trying to cash it out through OTC brokers and Chinese money laundering networks.

The firm also rejected the speculation that AI agents were doing the laundering.

"The laundering is clearly automated, but nothing we have seen shows an AI agent making decisions," Elliptic said.

On Friday, Binance wrote on X that it was working to reopen withdrawals for HYPE, INJ, APT, PLASMA, NEAR and LTC by the "end of next week." It is also aiming to reopen withdrawals "for all tokens on networks that are already restored," including ETH, BSC, Arbitrum and Optimism.

The exchange's own report, published Tuesday, said the attack was carried out over months and involved a tampered developer tool, a fake job interview and compromised internal AI tooling that got the attackers access to Binance's wallet-signing infrastructure.

Binance has since replenished virtually all the assets stolen in the breach.

Mississippi Senate: Longshot Ty Pinkins says he aimed to spoil Hyde-Smith, boost Colom vote

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Longshot Mississippi Senate candidate Ty Pinkins said that at first wanted to win — but that he stayed in to spoil.

Pinkins told a local TV station that after an April poll showed he had no chance of winning he remained in the race in order to boost Democratic nominee Scott Colom's chances of beating Republican incumbent Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith.

Pinkins' purported strategy might pay off.

Colom is narrowly leading Hyde-Smith in ongoing counting by just 1,936 votes, a margin of just 0.2 percentage points, with 96.2% of the vote counted, according to the Associated Press. That margin is about one-tenth the size of Pinkins' share of the vote, 2.1 percentage points.

Pinkins has won just 20,010 votes so far out of the nearly 952,000 votes already counted.

"The only thing I did was stay in the race and let people shoot arrows at me," Pinkins said in an interview with WTVA in Mississippi on Thursday. "That is it. And that's a ... low price to pay to get someone in this race that I think can help people in the Delta and all across this state."

"The portion of the vote that I drew — and people were concerned about splitting the Black vote — what I actually did was split the Republican vote," he said.

Pinkins told the station in Tupelo that he intentionally avoided campaigning in areas with large Black populations to avoid cutting into the vote share of Colom, who, like Pinkins, is Black.

That tactic worked, he said, as the election results show he got most of his support from areas with relatively low Black populations.

"I'll be the boogeyman," he said. "But I'll tell you this: The independent in this race pulled most of his votes from a Republican, and for the first time since Reconstruction, a Democrat who is a Black man is leading."

Pinkins, a lawyer who previously ran for Mississippi secretary of state, was the Democratic nominee for Senate in 2024. He was crushed by Republican Sen. Roger Wicker, who won nearly 63% of the vote, compared with about 37% for Pinkins.

Despite that showing, the U.S. Army veteran decided to take another crack at the Senate in 2026.

But in the spring, he saw the writing on the wall for his candidacy.

"I got in this race to win," Pinkins told WTVA. "In April, I knew I was going to lose, but I also knew that I was pulling 70% of the Republican vote based on the [SPLC Action Fund and Impact Research] poll."

SPLC Action Fund, when it released the poll, said, "Sagging favorability numbers for Sen. Cindy Hyde-Smith could open up a path to victory for Democrats."

"Independent Ty Pinkins has the potential to play spoiler for either candidate," SPLC Action Fund said in April.

In the WTVA interview, Pinkins said, "I probably attended maybe, maybe six events over the summer" as part of his campaign.

"Six events. I don't have to," Pinkins said. "Why? I'm not going to win. So why go to these Black communities that he's going to?"

Pinkins said all he had to do was visit counties that have relatively low Black populations and "talk to some Republicans that said they're going to vote for me."

"They were literally calling me and sending me emails: 'We're going to vote for you because we don't want to waste our vote on Cindy Hyde-Smith, we don't want to hold our nose and vote for her, so we're going to vote for you. Please stay in the race,'" he said.

The campaigns of Colom and Hyde-Smith did not immediately respond to CNBC's requests for comment.

9 Ways To Upgrade Your Burger King Breakfast Order

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

All too often we have to settle for a protein bar or a lackluster cup of coffee as a way to start the day. So, when we finally treat ourselves to a breakfast sandwich, pancakes, or some hash browns, they need to be good. Part of making that morning stop worth it is being able to customize the meal just the way you like it. So, if you're making the trip to Burger King for breakfast on the way to work, school, or a day out, here are some ways to up your order's flavor and value in a few quick steps.

And just to make things as easily understood as possible, consider going into the restaurant to order for any multi-step adjustments. It'll cut down on the confusion that can sometimes happen through an app. But however you choose to order, be sure to arrive before the 10:30 a.m. cutoff so you can get the breakfast you've been dreaming of.

Prices can vary from location to location. All pricing information in this article is from a San Diego, California Burger King. Please check the website or app for your local restaurant to confirm current costs.

Hash browns are already a crunchy part of Burger King's breakfast burritos, so why not carry that over to the sandwiches, too? It adds texture, flavor, and keeps you from having to reach into the bag over and over again. It's also a tip that can carry over to other establishments. For instance, you can use the spuds to elevate your next Egg McMuffin (or just about any other breakfast sandwich you'd like). At a San Diego BK location, a side of hash browns costs $3.29, just in case you want to add extra to what comes with a combo meal.

There are several ways to do this flavor hack, with some fans suggesting combining the meat and eggs from a breakfast sandwich on a burger with the tots. Others say to keep it simple and place a few of the crispy potatoes on a Croissan'wich with one, or a few types, of the many sauces available. But, really, however you feel you should be customizing the sandwich in the moment is the right choice.

If you're a fan of soft, sweet pancakes and sausage, bacon, or ham, why not combine them into the ultimate DIY breakfast sandwich? The restaurant's pancake platter makes this one of the easiest hacks overall, but the catch is not every location will have them. However, those getting breakfast in one that does can grab the platter and already have the components they need for an incredibly sweet and savory sausage sandwich. The diner can even decide whether they want to drizzle syrup on the pancakes, dunk them, or both.

It's easy to up the protein for more hearty options, too. If you don't happen to see the option to buy just an extra sausage patty or bacon on the app or in-store menu board, don't worry. A BK employee on Reddit said the restaurant can sell different individual ingredients, as long as the worker knows how to ring it up and what's currently available.

French toast sticks combine the crispiness of hash browns and the sweetness of syrup into an easy way to add both to your sandwich or burger in one move. An order is $3.29 at one San Diego location, so it likely won't put much of a dent in your wallet either. This might appear to be a questionable move, but fans of the convenience store Sheetz's French toast sticks already know using the bread as a way to complement eggs, bacon, cheese, and jalapeños, is a crowd-pleaser.

People praise Burger King's version of the crunchy pieces of toast, including some vegetarians and vegans who note the item is made without dairy or eggs. However, the company notes that the sticks might share equipment with dairy products in case you need to avoid it. For those who do put the toast on their sandwiches, the bread's crispy exterior combining with the other ingredients makes it a uniquely tasty meal you'll be sure to remember.

Want more flavor and a creamy texture added to your breakfast? Mix and match the many Burger King sauces and jams for unexpectedly delicious upgrades to the burritos, burgers, and sandwiches. Condiments like the picante sauce, barbecue sauce, honey mustard, and grape or strawberry jelly can be spread directly onto the item, used as a dip, or mixed together to do both. Many of them don't cost extra, but the ones that do are less than a quarter at a BK location in San Diego.

A Reddit user posted about their order, saying, "Did the mix and match double sausage croissan'wiches, added the strawberry jam to get a little sweet with all this savory!" Others suggest using ranch to compliment the meal, which is a solid idea because it's herby tang can cut through a bit of grease if necessary. But the ever-dependable ketchup remains on stand-by, too. So, let your tastebuds lead you to creating the perfect mix of flavors for your next breakfast outing.

If you like milk tea and/or iced coffee, this hack is an easy way to meet in the middle by making a pseudo-affogato. An affogato is traditionally made by pouring a shot of espresso over gelato to create a beautiful union of extreme temperatures and taste differences. If you're close to a location that offers ice cream, you can add some BK soft serve to your hot or cold coffee for a sweet and creamy start to the day. At one San Diego restaurant, the hot, brewed coffee cost $2.89 per cup, while the iced coffees are priced at $3.39.

In addition to upping the flavor, this upgrade will also give the coffee a silky quality from the cold dairy. It's a quick way to cool down the hot kind and to sweeten the cold variety, thanks to the soft serve already containing cream, sugar, and vanilla. All in all, this is a great way to have something unique without having to wait in line at a specialty coffee house. We'll call that an early morning win.

For those watching their macros, or if you're just craving some extra meat, adding a burger patty to any of the breakfast sandwiches will give you flame-broiled flavor and lots of protein. Many Burger King locations sell all of its hamburgers during breakfast, so there's no shortage of the patties being cooked. But if you want an extra fresh and hot one, asking for the beef to be "fresh off the broiler" will get you a cooked-to-order patty.

To further customize the sandwich (and add even more protein), some customers combine the contents of a Croissan'wich with a burger. To make the process even easier, diners can order the Fully Loaded Croissan'wich, which comes with bacon, sausage, ham, eggs, and American cheese on buttery bread, and add a hamburger patty to the whole thing or sub out one of the other meats for it. The fully loaded croissant option costs $7.19 at a San Diego location, but the price of an extra patty isn't advertised on the restaurant's website.

Eggs might not sound exciting to everyone, but the soft, unctuous items can easily become the star of the meal on a DIY breakfast burger. Putting an egg, or in this case a BK egg patty, on a regular burger adds flavor, texture, and protein while keeping the sandwich from becoming one-ingredient-too-many. That might have something to do with the way the folded item can take on flavor while staying undeniably eggy. That sponge-like ability allows the egg to take on a sauce, cheese, and beef or plant-based burger and bring out the best in all of them.

The chain has tested the idea of a proprietary breakfast burger called the Bacon Cheddar Hash Whopper that included an egg patty and hollandaise aioli. It was only available briefly in select markets, so those who enjoy this hack will have to keep waiting to order it without asking for modifications to a regular burger.

Eggs aren't just unique in their flavor properties, but their ability to change textures depending on how they're cooked is incredible. They can be so soft they're hard to keep on a fork or so tough they can feel hard to chew through. But there's a middle ground where eggs stay light and fluffy while staying strong enough to keep their shape. That area is what makes taking the bread off a breakfast sandwich or burger and using egg patties for a bun possible. Plus, this is a great way to get rid of carbs while adding protein and a silky consistency.

To get those egg patties at the restaurant, you'll have to ask to double the eggs on your sandwich while requesting no bread. Just know the patty will cost a little extra. You can also order a double meat sandwich and ask to swap a sausage with another egg to make it even easier.

While there are plenty of social media food hacks to avoid (anyone else remember the toaster grilled cheese trend?), there are others that are absolutely inspiring. Using hash browns to make breakfast sandwiches is one. And don't forget the pancakes as a bun, either. All you have to do for this hack is cut them into quarters to have sweet and savory treats in a bite-size form.

So, whether diners want to make their own silver-dollar-esqe sandwiches or use French toast or hash browns for crunchy mini-stacker breakfast versions, this upgrade lets customers fully customize their morning meal while playing with their food. Both the French toast sticks and the hash browns cost $3.29 per order at a BK location in San Diego, but you'll have to inquire at the restaurant for pricing on extra eggs, meat, and/or cheese. Whatever combination you decide on, feel free to mix it up even further by grabbing a few different sauces to complete this DIY upgrade.

The inside track on Celtic's squad spirit and pressure cooker every three days

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Mika Baur is discovering that life at Celtic rarely offers a moment to catch your breath. The expectations are relentless, the schedule unforgiving and the emotional swings can be considerable.

But if Thursday’s crucial 3-2 Europa League comeback victory over Besiktas demonstrated anything, it is that there remains plenty of belief within the Celtic dressing room. After a difficult period, Martin O’Neill’s side retuned to winning ways by recording their first three points of the competition’s league phase at the fourth attempt.

Did you know with a Digital Subscription to The Scotsman, you can get unlimited access to the website including our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

For German midfielder Baur, the result was particularly welcome after a bleak run of form. In a contest that demanded character as much as quality, Celtic found it through a blend of steely determination, attacking flair and the influence of their substitutes.

The 22-year-old believes the manner of the win over their Turkish opponents outlined Celtic’s ability to respond to adversity, although he recognises that life in Glasgow rarely offers time to dwell on either success or disappointment.

“The win was important after some tough weeks,” he declared. “But the belief and the way we work together as a team has always been good. There was never any problem with that because we always believed in ourselves. We showed how we can leave our hearts on the field and put in a shift. It was important to get those three points and we deserved it. Hopefully we can go forward with confidence from this game.”

The response to successive Premiership setbacks against Dundee United and Falkirk was particularly important given the standards expected at Celtic Park. Baur has quickly learned there is little room for inconsistency at a club where winning is a must at all costs, irrespective of the opposition or competition.

“There is always pressure to win here, to win every three days,” Baur admitted. “That’s what we all want. In the last two games it wasn’t possible for us. We knew it wasn’t good enough but here we played well and deserved it.

“The fans helped a lot. You could feel the energy. It was not just us players on the field who deserved the win – it was also everyone around the stadium and they brought the energy as well. It’s an unbelievable feeling as a player to have everyone pushing you and being there for you. It gives you that last percentage of energy you need at the end.

“Playing in this unbelievable stadium in front of these fans, you feel the pressure with some of the games we have lost but I am getting used to it. You don’t get much time.”

A decisive factor in Celtic's comeback was the game-changing contribution of Camilo Duran and Callum Osmand. Introduced from the bench, both players injected fresh energy into Celtic’s attack, helping turn the game in their favour, with the Colombian scoring twice and Osmand providing a goal and an assist.

Baur believes their performances underlined the importance of squad depth and the collective responsibility required to deliver success.

“That’s what you want from the subs – to make an impact from the bench,” he explained. “It says a lot for Camilo to accept the situation of starting on the bench, but to come on and make such an impact, producing in the big moments to score those two goals to win us the game. Ossie as well, he came on with a goal and assist. It’s not just the goals and assists, Ossie won the ball as well and was working hard. I’m really glad for them.”
