Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

India’s Enforcement Directorate (ED) has searched premises linked to crypto exchanges CoinDCX and Delta Exchange in Bengaluru. The agency alleges unauthorised cross-border transactions in crypto derivatives and tokenised assets worth thousands of crores.

The ED’s Bengaluru Zonal Office searched three premises on November 4 and 5 under Section 37 of the Foreign Exchange Management Act (FEMA), 1999, according to a statement from the agency.

The ED said the case began with information alleging large-scale FEMA violations by two companies:

According to the ED, both platforms offered crypto futures and options, trading in real-world asset (RWA) tokens and tokenised stocks of foreign companies. The agency said they openly advertised tokenised foreign stock trading even though they were not authorised to offer it. One CoinDCX advertisement promoted “Trade in US Stock Futures” starting at ₹100.

How The Alleged Spot Trading Scheme Worked

The ED described what it called “double hop” spot trading transactions on CoinDCX:

The agency alleged that these transfers bypassed the framework for cross-border money transfers. It said the trades ran through an account held in the name of a Costa Rica-based entity, which it described as a “paper entity”, and were carried out directly from CoinDCX through application programming interfaces (APIs).

According to the ED, the volume of these cross-border spot transactions runs into thousands of crores.

Futures And Options Allegedly Mirrored Abroad

The ED alleged that CoinDCX’s futures and options products were also executed on foreign platforms, including Binance, Bybit, KuCoin and Gate.io. According to the agency:

The ED said these cross-border futures and options transactions also run into thousands of crores.

The searches come weeks after CoinDCX faced scrutiny over a deposit wallet incident that led to losses of about $16 million. Blockchain investigator ZachXBT had accused the exchange of initially denying that the loss had taken place.

The agency’s investigation is ongoing, and the allegations have not been proven.

The Iambic Model T Is the Luxury Sneaker Built for Life at the Racetrack

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Our car experts choose every product we feature. We may earn money from the links on this page.

Italian leather, custom comfort, and auto-inspired design make these my favorite shoes for Formula 1 weekends.

There’s something fitting about the Model T sneaker becoming my favorite for race weekends. Much like the cars we obsess over, the Iambic Model T takes an engineering-first approach to comfort, combining modern technology, thoughtful construction, and premium materials.

Think of it like commissioning a luxury grand tourer. A Ferrari isn’t simply about horsepower; it’s about the materials, the craftsmanship, and attention to the person behind the wheel. Iambic applies the same philosophy, from its Italian leather to its personalized fit.

After wearing mine throughout the year, across Europe and the U.S., I'm convinced the approach works.

Rather than relying on conventional shoe sizing, Iambic uses AI-assisted technology to create a personalized fit. The process begins with a smartphone app that captures images of your feet and collects information about your comfort preferences. It may seem unusual, but I promise it makes sense. Through the app, you will send pictures of each foot from different angles to capture the full scope and how you stand. That data helps determine how your shoes are made. These foot scans showed me that I wear slightly more on my right side because of an old skating injury, something I never knew.

The Model T is handcrafted in Portugal using Italian full-grain leather, with a triple-layer rubber sole, adjustable foam insoles, and a padded heel collar. It’s an impressive combination.

For someone accustomed to appreciating the details inside a well-appointed Italian automobile, that approach feels familiar. The materials matter, but so does how everything works together.

I’ve worn my white Model Ts all over Modena’s cobblestone streets with my family, through multiple days at racetracks, and across America on my 36-hour Route 66 road trip. They’ve become my favorite driving and racing shoes, and that says a lot.

Anyone who’s attended a race knows how much walking it involves. Between navigating sprawling circuits, standing for hours, and sometimes carrying equipment or bags, your footwear gets a serious workout.

Silverstone was the ultimate test. Four consecutive days of traversing the circuit left my sneakers considerably dirty, but my feet stayed unswollen and blister-free. I didn’t even wear socks with them the last day (so they wouldn’t clash with my Fernando outfit). They were so comfortable that I rarely thought about my feet, even when walking across the hilly, uneven grounds and up and down the grandstand.

They also became my preferred driving shoes for long-haul trips with my cousin. Their fit and manageable sole made hours and hours in the driver’s seat enjoyable, without the bulk of traditional athletic sneakers. And despite the punishment they underwent, a basic leather-cleaning kit brought the white back to life.

At $600, the Model T belongs in the same conversation as other premium footwear, but the appeal goes beyond appearance. You’re investing in personalized construction, a sneaker designed for serious use and only for you. After a full season of travel, mine have more than justified their price.

With Austin, Texas, behind us and Las Vegas approaching, remember that a Grand Prix involves considerably more walking than most first-time attendees anticipate. Every year, social media fills with photos of blistered feet from fans who chose fashionable boots or sleek loafers over comfort. My advice? Save the fancy boots and dress shoes for dinner.

If you’re already investing in the experience of attending a Formula 1 race, or any race, the right footwear makes perfect sense. Like a beautifully engineered Italian sports car, the Iambic Model T proves performance and luxury can coexist.

Will The Blue Wave Midterms Put A Damper On Ad Tech M&A?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

It’s been a hot year for mergers and acquisitions in ad tech and media. But the M&A trend might be due for a cooldown.

Between DoorDash-Instacart, Publicis-LiveRamp, Nielsen-DoubleVerify, Skydance-Paramount and Stagwell-Freestar, there’s been plenty to discuss regarding blockbuster consolidation. And how the political landscape affects such deals is always a prominent talking point.

The thinking goes that Republican administrations – particularly the Trump admin and his DOJ and FTC – are friendlier to mergers between big companies, whereas Democratic administrations apply more antitrust scrutiny.

Plus, regulatory concerns have hung over most of the major acquisitions this year.

The FTC approved Publicis acquiring LiveRamp, but on the condition that Publicis ensures neutrality for LiveRamp’s other customers. Skydance’s Paramount deal was dogged by accusations from Democrats that the companies were bribing President Trump to get the deal approved. And Nielsen faces its own antitrust lawsuit that’s gaining steam.

Meanwhile, DoorDash CEO Tony Xu confirmed that he backed away from a bid to acquire Instacart in 2021 over a perception that the Biden administration would kibosh the deal.

So what are the implications for M&A now that the midterm elections gave Democrats control of the House, the Senate and Congress’s vast investigative power? Will the lame duck portion of the second Trump term be less conducive to consolidation?

The AdExchanger team weighs in on whether this week’s M&A activity – Affle buying Perion for $420 million and Chord acquiring Zenlytic for $5 million – might be the last gasp of a dying trend, or whether there’s still time for more wheeling and dealing.

And, with the midterm elections now behind us, that means sports fans will get a break from the $12 billion onslaught of political advertising. But it also means more room for a $7 billion blitz of gambling and prediction market ads.

AdExchanger’s James Hercher recently penned an opinion column on how the live sports ad experience has felt extra exploitative lately. Call it the enshittification of sports.

His take is that pro leagues and broadcasters are putting marketing dollars above fan enjoyment and even player safety. And he’s got plenty of receipts.

Agentic media buying is the No. 1 topic du jour in ad tech discussion circles. But how does agentic buying even work when it comes to buying TV and streaming ads? And how does that workflow differ from programmatic? The startup Swivel aims to address those questions.

AppLovin is growing at a blistering pace, but Wall Street investors remain cautious about the business.

High-end ad blockers mimic some of the same behavior as bot traffic. So WiseGuyGaming repurposed the tools it was already using to identify bots to reveal its ad-blocking audience as well.

Criteo has weathered considerable storms in its day, including a painful moment in March 2020 when its market cap bottomed out below $500 million. Can it pull off yet another turnaround?

Disaffection and outright anger toward US sports institutions run deeper than logo-cluttered fields. Leagues and teams are sacrificing player safety and fan enjoyment for marketing dollars.

People Inc.’s referral traffic from Google search is down 70% over the past two years, and by 22% in Q3 alone. But its digital revenue is still growing, albeit slowly.

Real-time strategy game The Fertile Crescent 2 is an isometric odyssey inspired by Age of Empires, with the 1.0 release out now

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Time to power-rank the biggest Bronze Age civs

Age of Empires-influenced real-time strategy game The Fertile Crescent 2: Collapse of the Bronze Age leaves early access today, publishers MicroProse have announced, with the 1.0 version adding the final chapters of the singleplayer story. You'll also get your grubby fingers on the very same map editor developers Wield Interactive used to design the campaign.

I'm new to The Fertile Crescent 2 myself. I do like the sound of a strategy sim in which you guide a fugitive community through the Late Bronze Age Collapse. The land has been thrown into turmoil by the arrival of the Sea People, this being a loose historical term for assorted ocean-going migrants who, I gather, are often blamed for the crumbling of the Hittites among other once-glorious Bronze Age powers. I don't have much scholarly insight to share - I thought the "Sea People" referred to expansionist mermaids, at first - but I do have a trailer.

Nate Crowley (RPS in peace) was exceedingly fond of the original game, calling it "a bite-sized chunk of phenomenal RTS design" that "manages to riff nostalgically on Age Of Empires, while completely rewriting its ground rules". The sequel continues with the same core concepts – above all, the paramount importance of food, which determines population and army size together with your civ's progression.

"This is not a game about building the biggest empire," the developers explain in a press release. "Resources have to be managed, stability is fragile, and growth comes with risk. Every expansion, every battle, and every decision must be weighed against survival. Building and managing a sustainable economy is as important as commanding your forces in battle. Expanding your army too quickly can put your food economy under pressure."

The story spans 17 missions, with four playable factions: the Assyrians, Egyptians, Babylonians, and Hittites. The 1.0 release also includes some standalone scenario missions, a skirmish mode, and multiplayer on dedicated servers with bot support.

Read more on Steam. If you're partial to historical strategy and don't mind winding back the clock (upturning the hourglass? Adjusting the sundial? Recalibrating the henge?) another few thousand years, Folk Emerging is pretty terrific. If you like stories about migrant tribes, and you don't mind trading your Hittites for Vikings, you might get a kick out of The Banner Saga.

Fox wraps up its $22 billion Roku deal

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

No Hannity on your home screen, at least for now.

Days after American voters soundly rejected the narratives sold by the Fox Corporation's "news" division, the company has expanded its digital empire. It's now officially the owner of Roku, in a $22 billion deal announced in June. The five-month turnaround is unusually short for a merger of this size, but the Trump Justice Department cleared it nonetheless following a review.

Roku reaches over 100 million households globally, serving as a gateway to streaming platforms like Netflix, YouTube TV and Disney+. It also sells ads and subscription bundles, while owning another streaming service, Tubi. Ironically, Fox sold its Roku shares in 2020 to help fund its Tubi acquisition.

The closure follows reports that Fox News' viewership dropped 27 percent year over year in October. By comparison, CNN's viewership grew by 3 percent and MS NOW (formerly MSNBC) rose by 16 percent. However, Fox News' viewership is still higher than CNN and MS NOW's combined.

Roku founder Anthony Wood will join Fox's board. He'll co-lead Fox Digital Platforms, a new division overseeing Roku, Tubi, Fox One and other digital brands.

What this means for Roku owners isn't yet clear. But given the media empire's inclination toward right-wing politics, it's hard to imagine the company passing up the opportunity to deploy Roku as another megaphone. For what it's worth, Fox says Roku will remain an "open platform" for every content partner. However, the worry is less about what services the company removes and more about what gets promoted on the home screen.

Google Gemini Business Profile Tools Are Coming To Workspace via @sejournal, @MattGSouthern

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Webinar: 9 AI Workflows For Better Content. Nov 18, 2 PM ET.

Google says Gemini's Business Profile tools are coming to Workspace accounts and moves the Maps Message button. Its help page no longer lists region limits.

Google says businesses that manage their Business Profile with a Google Workspace account are getting access to Gemini’s profile tools. Its latest Small Business Bulletin says that access is rolling out now.

In contrast to the bulletin, Google’s help page regarding the Gemini connection shows work accounts still can’t be used. Google has removed the exclusion for the European Economic Area and the U.K. from the list of unsupported areas since Oct. 13.

Lisa Landsman, Google’s Head of Industry Engagement & SMB Success, posted the bulletin in the Business Profile Community on Nov. 5.

The same bulletin states that businesses who have established a chat option on their Business Profile now display a “Message” button next to the “Call” button on their Google Maps listings on both Android and iOS devices. Businesses may choose to provide a link to customers to send messages via WhatsApp. However, a dead link or unread inbox now sits in one of the top buttons displayed when a customer clicks on your Google Maps listing.

The bulletin labels Workspace access “rolling out now” for the Business Profile connection Google announced in June. Instead of an individual Gmail account, businesses can soon use a corporate Workspace email to link a verified Business Profile (through Gemini), Google says. Currently, a Workspace admin needs to enable Gemini app access within the Admin console before employees will be able to link.

Owners can also upload photos and videos into Gemini chat directly from a device or from Google Photos, Google says, allowing them to add images to their Business Profile photo or create new posts. Examples of prompts include setting your special hours for Black Friday.

The help center indicates that as of Nov. 6 owners need to log in using a personal Google Account, and it also states, “For now, this feature isn’t available if you sign in with a work or school Google Account.” Additionally, the media section does describe how users can upload images from a public URL, but it doesn’t indicate that users can load files from their device.

A previously archived version of the same help page dated Oct. 13 shows that at that time the Gemini connection was not available in the European Economic Area (EEA), nor the United Kingdom; in addition, 12 different languages were supported. However, the current help page does not list either restriction; it simply states, “Available wherever the Gemini web app is supported.” The most recent edition of Google’s newsletter does not address any region or language restrictions.

Google still restricts linking to an owner or manager of a single verified Business Profile, and only through the Gemini web app.

Landsman noted that Google has moved the Business Profile Message button higher up on Google Maps listings:

We’ve expanded the feature so customers on both Android and iOS can now find a prominent Message button right next to “Call” on your Google Maps listing. Previously, finding chat on Maps required scrolling deep down into your profile. Now, whether a customer views your profile, opens the expanded header, or uses the bottom action bar, messaging is front and center.

Tapping the button sends customers to the chat option the business has set up, including WhatsApp and text, according to the newsletter. Google’s chat help page lists those two options and says a profile with both set shows customers only the text option. The same page says chat options are available in select regions, without naming them, and might not be available for every profile.

Business Profile’s hours editor now has a “Generate hours” section, where owners type their schedule in plain language and Google fills in the hours fields. Google’s examples include appointment-only days and a lunch closing.

Google’s hours help page explains that the tool is available in select regions and languages, and owners can review and edit the generated hours before saving them.

Restaurants may turn off certain third-party ordering services by using the Business Profile dashboard (as I reported on Oct. 28). According to Google’s ordering help page, an order service is only hidden entirely when it’s turned off for both pickup and delivery.

In addition to Vegetarian and Vegan labels, there is a menu tag option for Dairy-Free and Gluten-Free labels. In addition, there are Kosher and Halal labeling options, which according to Google should only be used where certified.

Before submitting an appeal, you can upload supporting documentation into the appeals tool. As stated in Google’s help page, evidence is optional, and you can only submit an appeal once.

Currently, the Gemini connection doesn’t work for your agency’s multiple client profiles with a single login; you have to be managing just one verified profile. As of the new help page (that doesn’t exclude businesses in the EEA and the U.K.), businesses in these regions can check if they see their connection.

Before the holiday rush, send a test message through the chat link on every profile you manage.

Google’s bulletin doesn’t specify when Workspace access will reach every account. Keep an eye on the Gemini help page for updates to its personal-account rule or one-profile limit.

OpenAI Introduces BioBasicsBench to Test AI Agents on Wet-Lab Data

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

OpenAI introduced BioBasicsBench on November 6, 2026, a 100-task benchmark developed with enzyme-design company Ligo to test whether AI agents can interpret raw evidence from routine wet-lab experiments.

OpenAI reports results as mean reward, the average task score expressed as a percentage of available credit. At xhigh, the highest reasoning-effort setting reported for all seven models tested, GPT-6 Astra reached 66.7% with a 95% confidence interval of 60.3–73.1%, while GPT-5.6 Sol scored 43.8% with an interval of 37.6–50.0%. Astra averaged 4,704 output tokens per task, compared with 9,099 for Sol.

BioBasicsBench addresses a limitation OpenAI identifies in evaluations built around charts and images selected for scientific papers: everyday lab work also involves failed experiments, ambiguous images, conflicting observations and troubleshooting. Its tasks ask agents to interpret experimental data, spot errors and recognize when the evidence cannot justify a conclusion. They span protein methods, imaging and cytometry, quantification and assays, nucleic acid methods and molecular cloning.

Scientists created the tasks from their own experiments and wrote grading rubrics. Inputs include gel and blot images, colony plates, microscopy stacks, sequencing reads, plate-reader exports, bench photographs and protocol videos. Individual tasks can combine several evidence types and supporting reference sheets, such as ladder charts and plasmid maps. Agents return structured conclusions, measurements or recommendations, and rubrics award credit for elements including numerical results, supporting reasoning and recognition that the evidence is insufficient.

Each task received an internal review by a Ligo scientist who had not written it, an external review by a PhD-level wet-lab scientist and a final internal review covering the prompt, evidence, rubric, author notes and a graded model attempt. The evaluation covered 37 model and reasoning-effort settings, with five attempts on every task per setting, producing 18,500 results. Confidence intervals were calculated by resampling whole tasks while keeping each task’s five attempts together.

In a cell-imaging task, GPT-5.6 Sol classified cells as dead from a dye signal. Astra recognized that overlapping detection windows could allow interference from a second dye and called for controls that could separate the signals. In a DNA-cloning task, Sol based its conclusions on seeing no colonies on a negative-control plate, whereas Astra noticed that the plate was labeled for kanamycin rather than the ampicillin used in the experiment and rejected it as a reliable control.

Another task showed cells moving in and out of focus. Sol sought a biological explanation for their apparent disappearance, while Astra identified a z-stack, a series of images captured at different depths. OpenAI interprets these examples as evidence of better multimodal handling, use of experimental context and recognition of what data can and cannot establish, and as an indication that stronger native multimodal capabilities may be important for routine wet-lab analysis.

OpenAI cautions that higher scores do not yet amount to reliable laboratory judgment. In a colony-PCR task, Astra selected lanes 4, 6, 7 and 8 after assuming that a larger PCR product indicated successful cloning; the reference interpretation selected lanes 5, 9 and 10. That mistake would lead to purification of the incorrect plasmid.

Other examples show correct observations failing to govern the final answer. Astra generalized that vacuoles, compartments inside cells, are generally darker than surrounding cell material despite lighter vacuoles in the same image. Changing focal depth can reverse the contrast, and Astra’s own intermediate observations noted that contrast varied with focus. In a colony-counting task, OpenAI reports that Astra failed to separate close neighbors, missed visible colonies and marked empty agar, producing an approximately 12% overcount relative to the benchmark reference. With plated volume and dilution fixed, such an overcount inflates the estimated efficiency of DNA introduction by the same proportion.

OpenAI says following the development of these capabilities will also inform its assessment of their biosafety and biosecurity consequences.

The practical bar is therefore higher than a better aggregate score: an agent must carry accurate observations through to its conclusion, check controls and withhold conclusions the evidence cannot support. BioBasicsBench makes those requirements targets for improvement, and its worked failures show why progress toward laboratory assistance still depends on closing the gap between observing evidence and making a sound experimental decision.

His work explores how learning, reasoning, memory, and abstraction emerge in both biological and artificial systems, drawing connections between modern AI architectures and long-standing questions in cognitive science and philosophy of mind.

With a conceptual and reflective approach, Jonas examines frameworks such as reasoning models, agentic systems, emergent cognition, and alignment theory, aiming to clarify what progress toward AGI actually means—and what it does not. Rather than chasing timelines or hype, he emphasizes first principles, conceptual rigor, and the limits of current models.

The EU accuses TikTok's 'rabbit hole' algorithm of breaching its content rules

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The bloc said the platform needs to change how the For You page works to help protect users’ mental health.

The European Commission has accused TikTok of breaching the Digital Services Act (DSA) over the potentially harmful effects of the recommendation system it uses on the For You page. The European Union's regulatory arm said the personalized nature of the algorithm means that the more a user interacts with a certain kind of content, the more they see similar videos.

"This design can be harmful when such endless and repeated exposure involves, for example, content about sadness, isolation or body image," the Commission said. "These 'rabbit holes' can have a detrimental impact on users' mental health, especially that of vulnerable adults and minors."

In its preliminary findings, the regulator said TikTok didn't do enough to assess how the algorithm could draw users into these rabbit holes and put their wellbeing at risk. Nor did it put sufficient measures in place to mitigate those risks, the Commission said, noting that when a user taps the "not interested" button on a video, that "may not be enough to change the content feed."

It said TikTok has to change the design of the For You page, and that it should "rely more on users' explicit preferences, disable viewing time as an indicator to recommend content, increase the accessibility and usability of user controls, and regularly test its algorithm." If TikTok doesn't sufficiently resolve the matter in the Commission's eyes and the EU confirms the findings, parent company ByteDance could be on the hook for a fine of as much as six percent of its annual global revenue.

TikTok has refuted the claims. "The Commission has made a series of statements that are fundamentally inaccurate and distort our platform's architecture and our commitment to user safety," it ​said in a statement to Reuters. "We have a strong record of protecting our community while giving users direct and intuitive ​control over their content experience."

The preliminary findings were issued as part of a larger EU investigation into TikTok that began in early 2024. In February, the bloc said TikTok breached the DSA because of the app's "addictive design." Officials also took TikTok to task in July, claiming it failed to protect the privacy of minors' accounts.

Regulators around the world have placed social media companies under closer scrutiny in recent years, particularly over the impact their platforms have on children. This week, Google, Meta and TikTok sued California over a law that bans social media companies from allowing users under 16 to access potentially "addictive features."

Want Better Margaritas? Add 5 Drops Of This Cheap Ingredient

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The margarita is a perennial cocktail favorite, mixing together tequila with sugar, lime, and often orange liqueur into a crowd-pleasing drink. Like many classic beverages, the formula opens the door to abundant fine-tuning, with subtle twists prominently impacting the flavor. Most often, home bartenders focus on details like the spirit used, ratios, and creative add-ins. But don't sleep on a trusty flavor enhancer already stocked in your kitchen: salt.

Salting a margarita rim is common, but there's even more benefit to mixing the mineral right into the cocktail. Much like in cooking, salt plays an important role in a drink's character. The ingredient enhances zesty notes (terrific with lime!) and balances sweet flavors, all while letting pleasant spirit flavors come to the forefront. In order to measure the amount of liquid seasoning, bartenders use a saline solution, which typically consists of four parts water to one part salt by weight, dissolved together.

Then, you can simply add four or five drops of the saltwater to your drink, and you'll get a sharper-tasting margarita. The drink even takes on a subtle textural enhancement, boasting a softer mouthfeel. For an ingredient found in nearly every pantry, that's a surprisingly easy way to improve the classic drink.

Saline drops don't just mesh with any margarita recipe, but are worth considering in all versions. Whether you're making a spicy rendition of the cocktail, trading blanco tequila for mezcal, or adding orange juice to the mix, the salt won't clash. Akin to how bitters are a cornerstone of many sweet and boozy drinks, saline solution deserves a place in every shaken margarita.

Naturally, you can adjust the amount added. If you want the drink's sweet flavors front and center, add only two drops or so. Alternatively, riffs that need a salty balance — say, an extra-tart spinoff with both cranberry and orange juice — can welcome as many as seven drops.

And don't rule out experimenting with varying types of salt. Sea salt can add subtle mineral notes, while smoked salt is a savvy way to imbue a subtle kiss of campfire depth. You can even dissolve ingredients right into the saline solution itself; bartenders incorporate everything from soy sauce to a touch of MSG. No need to get fancy, though. If you're assembling a drink at the last minute, even a classic, undissolved dash of salt can work its magic in a margarita, proving the effectiveness of this ingredient.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

Writer by choice, CryptoCurrency Writer, and Researcher by chance. Currently, focusing on financial news and analysis, as well as cryptocurrency news and data. One may not call me a crypto “Enthusiast” but trust me I'm getting there.

XRP fell 7.3% over the past 24 hours to $1.19, breaking below a support level that had held through weeks of market turbulence, according to market data. The token is down 4.6% over the past seven days.

The decline came as a broad sell-off in US technology stocks spilled into cryptocurrency markets. Until this week, XRP had held up better than many rival tokens and the S&P 500.

The token slipped below the $1.21 to $1.22 range, which analysts had identified as strong support. According to an analyst who tracks XRP’s price structure, two technical markers met in that range: the 61.8% Fibonacci retracement of the token’s previous rally and the 100% extension of its latest pullback.

The same outlook had warned before the drop that a bottom had not been confirmed. Recent rebounds had come in three-leg moves, a pattern that often signals a correction rather than a sustained recovery.

Charts point to two main levels below the current price.

The first is $1.10, which matches the 78.6% Fibonacci retracement. That level should ideally hold if the broader bullish setup is to stay intact. A decisive break below it would sharply reduce the chances of a rebound.

The second is the August swing low near $1. A fall below that level would fully invalidate the bullish setup and open the way to a deeper decline.

On the upside, the former support at $1.21 to $1.22 may now act as resistance.

A move above $1.35, the token’s most recent swing high, would be the first sign that a bottom has formed. That would also need to come with a sustained five-leg rally on shorter timeframes, which has not yet appeared.

Beyond that, $1.65 is the next major hurdle. A break above it would put the $2.60 area in view.

XRP edged up 0.8% in the past hour as buyers stepped in after the decline. The token’s direction in the near term is likely to depend on whether it can hold $1.10 while risk appetite remains weak across stocks and digital assets.

Tesla "winding down" production of Cybertruck

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

According to a recent tweet by Tesla founder Elon Musk, the electric vehicle company will be decreasing production of the Cybertruck at its Texas Gigafactory, just two and a half years after the car’s debut.

Musk said on Friday morning that Cybertruck production will be “winding down” to “make room” for the Cybercab, an electric sedan Tesla unveiled in 2024.

The change will go into effect starting mid-2027, according to Musk. It is unclear if the Cybertruck will be completely discontinued, although Musk agreed with a user of Musk-owned social media platform X, who called the news an “end of an era”.

“True, but the Cybertruck fleet will be around for a long time”, the Tesla founder replied.

Tesla will be winding down production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year to make room for increased Cybercab production

The Texas Gigafactory outside of Austin is the only factory known to produce the Cybertruck, according to MSN.

The shift may be due to poor sales of the vehicle since it was delivered to customers starting in late 2023.

The company sold 20, 237 Cybertrucks in the US last year, down from 38,965 in 2024, representing a 48 per cent drop, according to an estimate from Kelley Blue Book.

Musk estimated the company would sell an average of between 250,000 and 500,000 trucks annually at a 2023 shareholders’ meeting.

The Cybertruck design was first unveiled in 2019. It quickly divided opinion for its boxy shape and sci-fi appearance.

It was launched and placed in showrooms in 2023. Shortly after, many models were recalled in 2024 due to faulty pedals.

Tesla has been shifting its factory lines in recent years, seemingly to usher in the production of its semi-autonomous products.

One example is the Cybercab due to be folded into the company’s robotaxi service.

Earlier this year, Tesla announced it shifted production lines for its Model S and Model X vehicles into fabrication of its Optimus humanoid robot, which recently went into production in California.

The company also debuted a flying Roadster concept car to much fanfare last month.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Pokemon Center has started selling music box dioramas and framed maps for all nine of the franchise’s regions, but you’re going to have a tough time getting your hands on them.

For as long as Pokemon has been around, it’s had some of the best merchandise in gaming. Not even counting the unbelievable amount of gorgeous and sought-after cards for the TCG, there are skateboard decks, Lego sets, plushes, golden figures, playsets, and oh so much more. Just look at the branded cans of soup, if you somehow need more proof.

Since there's clearly no shortage of Pokemon goods in the world already, you’d think that there’s not much left that could possibly leave an impression. Well, you might want to change that tune, as the Pokemon Center has just revealed an incredible new collection of dioramas and framed maps that are going to leave you cursing if you’re not in Japan.

As pointed out by Dexerto, the official Japanese Pokemon site recently shared a blog post detailing a new collection that’s being added to the online store on November 12. The collection focuses on the “evolution of the Pokemon series” and includes music box dioramas representing locations from each region in the series, framed maps, and paper crafts.

Watching thousands of Pokemon Go players gather in Chicago showed me that the game's greatest feature has always been community.

Pokemon is no stranger to dioramas, but these music boxes are absolutely gorgeous, featuring nicely crafted replicas of areas like Route 1 alongside acrylic or 3D figures of the respective generation’s starter Pokemon and, of course, Pikachu. It’s really just the cherry on top that they also serve as a music box.

My favourite of the bunch has to be Route 3 from Sword and Shield, which has a lovely figure of my fave Scorbunny.

Those dioramas are selling for 12,100 yen, which equates to around $76, so they’re not exactly cheap, but they are very cool. The framed maps are equally gorgeous and going for around $105, while the paper crafts are just over $24. Of course, there’s a big catch to all of this, as the items are all exclusive to the Japanese Pokemon Center Online store.

Like with all Pokemon Center Online merchandise, there’s a chance that we see it come to other parts of the world later down the line. Until then, though, you’re either going to have to figure out a workaround or dare to look into the depths of reselling and scalpers. That’s just another day ending in Y for Pokemon fans, though.

Pokemon has entertained both children and adults alike for several generations. Whether hit video games, anime series and movies, trading cards, or more, there's something for every budding trainer hoping to catch 'em all.

Amazon's latest Alexa for Shopping feature allows you to text the AI agent with your shopping requests. It's available to select customers in the US now ahead of a wider rollout in the coming weeks.

You could already dump your grocery shopping list onto Alexa within the Alexa Shopping app or online, but Amazon says the new texting option is the most convenient solution yet. You can text to find out the status of an upcoming delivery, and any requests carry over to the Amazon Shopping app and the website, if you want to do more research.

To be among the first people to test the new feature, Amazon is inviting sign-ups here. You'll need to link your phone number, and if accepted you'll see "Alexa Shopping" in your contacts.

Free transfer signing is rediscovering his best form at Watford: Opinion

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Watford have started this season poorly under Alessio Dionisi. Despite promising signs to start the year, Dionisi’s side have been stuck in a rut with led to talk of yet another managerial sacking from the Hornets.

Watford, who host Middlesbrough this weekend, need inspiration from somewhere as they look to climb away from the wrong end of the Championship table, and Myron Boadu could be providing exactly that.

The Dutch striker arrived at Vicarage Road on a free transfer in September after a difficult spell at PSV, but recent performances suggest he is beginning to rediscover the qualities that once made him such an exciting prospect.

His brace against Norwich City on October 31 was followed by an assist in the 1-0 victory over Lincoln City three days later. More importantly, these contributions have come alongside encouraging performances in other areas of his game.

Boadu is looking sharper, making intelligent runs and showing the work rate that made him so effective earlier in his career. There is a reason Boadu was once valued at more than €40m and attracted a €17m move to Monaco.

He scored 32 league goals in 64 appearances for AZ Alkmaar before enjoying another productive spell on loan at Bochum, where he registered nine goals in 19 Bundesliga appearances. Injuries and inconsistent opportunities subsequently disrupted his progress, but the underlying talent has never disappeared.

His early Watford numbers are encouraging. Across seven Championship appearances and 379 minutes, Boadu has scored twice and provided one assist, averaging approximately 0.7 goal involvements per 90 minutes, according to FotMob.

His 1.40 expected goals compared with two actual goals also suggests he is taking his opportunities well rather than relying on an unsustainable volume of chances.

There are encouraging signs beyond his attacking output, too. Boadu has registered five tackles, nine recoveries and 18 duels won, while recording 20 touches inside the opposition penalty area. These figures reflect a forward becoming increasingly involved in both Watford’s pressing game and their attacking transitions.

His developing partnership with Iker Bravo is another significant positive. Boadu’s understanding with the fellow forward appears to be growing, with their movement complementing one another without requiring extensive preparation.

The Norwich performance demonstrated their potential, while his contribution against Lincoln showed he can also influence games through his work without necessarily finding the net himself. Of course, seven appearances do not represent a complete revival, and Boadu still needs to deliver consistently over a longer period.

However, Watford cannot afford to overlook the encouraging signs. If he maintains this intensity and continues building his understanding with Bravo, the Hornets could have a valuable attacking weapon for the remainder of the campaign.

GMX's USDG supply grew from around $1.5 million to nearly $17 million in less than a month.

The stablecoin is integrated into GM pools and the GMX Dollar Vault across BTC, ETH and SOL markets.

Arbitrum offers 200,000 ARB to each of the first 10 qualifying teams reaching an average $10 million USDG TVL.

GMX’s USDG expansion is giving Arbitrum another reason to court stablecoin builders. The USDG stablecoin supply reportedly climbed from around $1.5 million in early October to nearly $17 million in less than a month. That’s roughly 10-fold growth, and GMX says integrations across its trading pools and Dollar Vault helped drive the increase.

The growth follows USDG’s integration into GM pools and the GMX Dollar Vault. These products are designed to capture trading volume across BTC, ETH and SOL markets, giving the stablecoin a role within GMX’s trading ecosystem.

The jump is notable, but supply growth alone doesn’t tell the whole story. The next question is whether other protocols can build lasting demand around USDG rather than simply adding another stablecoin ticker.

Arbitrum’s Stablecoin Growth Program is targeting teams building products with USDG at their core. Support includes comarketing, technical integrations and foundation advisory.

Projects may also receive consideration for allocations under Arbitrum’s 165 million ARB incentive programs. There’s a more direct carrot, too: the first 10 teams to reach an average USDG total value locked (TVL) of $10 million can receive 200,000 ARB each, alongside TVL-based rewards.

Whether you are building lending markets, DEX pairs, yield strategies, or payments infrastructure, Arbitrum is deploying resources to back native $USDG adoption.Bring your contracts, scale your liquidity, and capture ecosystem rewards.👉 Apply here: https://t.co/lyqeoQODQW…

The program isn’t limited to projects already operating on Arbitrum One. Builders from other chains can also be considered, including early-stage teams without tens of millions in existing volume.

Arbitrum specifically points to lending markets, decentralized exchange pairs, yield strategies and payment infrastructure as potential use cases. Innovative vaults and automated market makers are also in scope.

The pitch is straightforward: bring contracts, build liquidity and tap ecosystem incentives. But incentives only get a project so far. Sustained usage has to follow.

For now, the USDG stablecoin has a growing footprint through GMX, while Arbitrum is offering funding and integration support to attract more builders. Whether that turns into durable adoption will depend on the products teams actually launch.

Le projet fou de Thomas Jolly pour Zinédine Zidane !

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Dans le numéro 241 du magazine So Foot paru en novembre 2026, Thomas Jolly a révélé le projet initial imaginé pour Zinédine Zidane lors de la cérémonie d'ouverture des Jeux olympiques de Paris 2024. Le directeur artistique prévoyait en effet une séquence spectaculaire au-dessus de la capitale.

Pour marquer les esprits au début du spectacle, Thomas Jolly avait initialement prévu de faire apparaître un hélicoptère dans la nuit parisienne. Une échelle devait descendre de l'appareil afin que Zinédine Zidane puisse s'y accrocher, flamme olympique à la main, pour survoler Paris jusqu'aux jardins des Tuileries.

Cette mise en scène aérienne avait été conçue comme un hommage direct à Jean-Paul Belmondo. Le directeur artistique souhaitait en effet rappeler sa célèbre cascade dans le film Le Guignolo, réalisé par Georges Lautner en 1980. Un clin d'œil cinématographique assumé.

Si cette cascade suspendue au-dessus de la ville pouvait sembler particulièrement périlleuse, Zinédine Zidane n'a pas hésité à valider ce plan. D'après les propos de Thomas Jolly dans les colonnes de So Foot, Zidane avait en effet donné son accord pour réaliser cette cascade aérienne lors de la cérémonie.

Toutefois, cette arrivée aérienne n'a jamais pu se concrétiser. Malgré l'accord donné par Zinédine Zidane, le cadre réglementaire est rapidement venu bloquer la mise en place de ce tableau pensé pour la soirée inaugurale.

Le projet a finalement dû être abandonné en raison de strictes contraintes de sécurité et des règles d'interdiction de survol d'un hélicoptère à basse altitude au-dessus de la capitale. Face à ces exigences, Thomas Jolly a dû renoncer à cette séquence.

Privé de cette séquence dans le ciel parisien, Thomas Jolly a donc revu le rôle de Zinédine Zidane pour la cérémonie. Le directeur artistique a imaginé un autre parcours afin de mettre en valeur sa présence au cœur de l'événement.

Lors de la cérémonie d'ouverture officielle du 26 juillet 2024, Zinédine Zidane est finalement apparu dans le film d'introduction. Il est ensuite monté sur la scène du Trocadéro sous la pluie pour transmettre la flamme olympique à Rafael Nadal.

Why didn’t the strong jobs report send mortgage rates skyrocketing?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The October jobs report showed 165,000 payroll growth and a steady 4.1% unemployment rate, consistent with improved 2026 labor conditions. Yield direction was driven more by AI-related market swings and broader bond market levels, keeping mortgage rates near 7.17%.  AI Summary

With all the crazy things happening this week, the blowout jobs report on Friday didn’t send mortgage rates up at all. But one thing is for sure: the labor market has been better in 2026 than it was in 2025. I once labelled the labor market the honey badger labor market and no matter what has been thrown at the U.S. economy after 2011 — outside the brief recession created by COVID-19 — the labor data has held up well.

Let’s dig into why very strong jobs report didn’t push yields or mortgage rates higher, as we would normally expect with the Fed being so hawkish in their statement.

From BLS:  Total nonfarm payroll employment rose by 165,000 in October, and the unemployment rate changed little at 4.1 percent, the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics reported today. Employment increased in professional and business services, in health care, and in transportation and warehousing.The labor data has improved enough that we have gone from a 4.5% unemployment rate last year to 4.1% today. Part of this is that labor-force growth has slowed and the break-even number has decreased, meaning fewer jobs are needed to keep the unemployment rate low. I have my break-even at 78,000 jobs created per month; I believe the Fed’s is 33,000. Some economists have it at zero, which I think is silly. In any case, the unemployment rate is lower this year versus last year.

Now, the low point in the COVID-19 recovery was about 3.4% unemployment back in 2023, so the unemployment rate has increased from that low, but it’s still good enough to keep the Fed’s focus on inflation instead of labor.

This month’s report had growth in a few sectors, especially the business services sector jobs with one of the biggest monthly prints in years. There was also growth in manufacturing, and transportation & warehouse, while construction jobs faded a tad.

Construction labor will be interesting in the future if mortgage rates stay elevated and short-term rates go up. If there is an AI slowdown and fewer AI data centers are being built, will this sector start to decline?

One of my most critical recession indicators, residential construction employment, doesn’t look great lately, but it isn’t looking terrible either. In previous cycles, you can see a clear downtrend in this labor pool, which hasn’t happened in this cycle. With builders doing more rate buydowns — which have kept new home sales at 2019 levels over the past few years — and remodeling work holding up, this clear recession indicator hasn’t broken yet.

As crazy as this week has been, the jobs data wasn’t the big driver of mortgage rates as much as AI stock prices. CoreWeave’s stock price was up 10% pre-market today, and bond yields ticked up with that. The jobs report didn’t do much to yields, we just saw a mild increase and then they fell back in line with the trend. Then CoreWeave stock price went almost negative, bond yields fell to the lows of the day, and now both have picked up from the lows! The market needs a weekend break.

Mortgage rates ended the day flat at 7.17%, so as crazy as this sounds, the blowout jobs data didn’t send mortgage rates or bond yields much higher. That’s good news for rates, and there are some levers the new Congress can pull to potentially get rates even lower, which I discussed on today’s episode of the HousingWire Daily podcast with Editor in Chief Sarah Wheeler.

A lot has changed this week for the economy regarding the bond market and where mortgage rates can go. The blue wave has raised hopes that the trade war and the conflict with Iran can end soon, AI companies have shown they can bleed, and Congress will try to slow the AI boom as much as it can without crashing it. All these things happened this week, so that even with four solid jobs reports during jobs week, the 10-year yield closed at 4.86% and mortgage rates closed at weekly lows.

TSMC is weighing a move into AI memory chips at Nvidia’s request

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The world's largest chipmaker is drafting plans for 3D DRAM as an AI-driven memory shortage drags on.

The TSMC logo on one of the company’s factories in the Central Taiwan Science Park, Taichung.

TSMC is considering making advanced memory chips and has begun drafting plans for 3D DRAM at Nvidia’s request, The Information reports. It would put TSMC up against Samsung, SK Hynix and Micron as AI demand keeps memory scarce.

TSMC is considering making advanced memory chips, Bloomberg reported on Friday, citing The Information. The move would give the world’s largest chipmaker an even bigger share of the AI hardware supply chain.

TSMC recently began drafting plans to make 3D DRAM, which stacks memory cells vertically to pack in more capacity, at the request of Nvidia, its biggest customer. It could build new factories or buy an existing plant and convert it, Reuters reported, citing The Information.

The work is led by Hsu Kuo-chin, a TSMC vice president who oversees advanced packaging, according to the report. TSMC and Nvidia did not immediately respond to requests for comment.

Memory is in short supply as AI data centres soak it up, sending prices soaring. Micron expects the shortage to last into 2028, and Apple raised Mac and iPad prices in June, citing memory costs. Some customers want their own supply: Elon Musk is planning a $55B chip plant to cut his reliance on Micron.

TSMC is already moving closer to memory. In October, Nvidia reportedly asked Samsung, SK Hynix and Micron to build the base layer of its custom HBM memory on TSMC’s 3nm process. Making DRAM itself would put TSMC up against all three, including SK Hynix, which plans to spend $720B on new plants.

The AI ‘credit card’ is getting costlier as CoreWeave bond yield tops 16%

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Debt markets are sending a message to AI data center builders: we're hiking the interest rate on your credit card and may put some new curbs on your credit limit.

That analogy comes courtesy of Jefferies global head of equity strategy, Christopher Wood, who put it in a note on Friday: "The most plausible way the AI capex arms race will end is that the credit card will be taken away."

That isn't happening yet, but credit markets have been spooked by the new political landscape — seeing more hurdles to data centers under Democratic governors in key states, along with a Democratic-controlled Congress.

The poster child for debt stress is neocloud Coreweave. The yield on its 2032 bond with a 9.625% coupon has climbed above 16%. Coreweave's five-year credit default swaps spiked above 1,289 basis points on Thursday, widening above their October peak, according to Deutsche Bank. The CDS eased 8% Friday to around 1,155.

"We've had plenty of warnings from the credit space," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note on Friday. Debt and equities are now moving more in sync, he added, "so we'll have to see if credit goes any further."

Investors are demanding more compensation to insure against default among even companies with the strongest balance sheets and credit ratings, including Nvidia, Broadcom, and Amazon.com.

CDS on Nvidia debt is up 76% in the last month. Broadcom's CDS is up 49%, while Amazon's has gained 44%. Their CDS remain low on an absolute basis; Nvidia's spread stood at roughly 139 basis points, compared with about 298 bps for Oracle and roughly 1,100 for CoreWeave. A large increase from a low starting point does not imply the same credit risk.

Still, rising CDS are a sign that markets want more protection against default among even the largest and healthiest debt issuers. And rising CDS imply higher financing costs down the line.

The market is now grappling with the prospect that at least some data center spending will be delayed or scrapped under incoming Democratic governors in Ohio, Texas, and Georgia, the three states with much of the planned capacity.

Moratoriums could impact 45% of planned capacity in the U.S. with a large amount of that in Texas, according to Goldman Sachs. Texas governor-elect Gina Hinojosa has said she's not opposed to data centers but wants a moratorium until the state legislature passes protections for communities.

None of these means AI debt financing is collapsing. Goldman tallies five AI bond deals totaling $67 billion since October 29, pushing total hyperscaler debt issuance this year to $296 billion. Goldman forecasts $440 billion of hyperscaler debt issuance next year, plus an additional $300 billion of project-level issuance across investment-grade and high-yield.

The Trump administration is vowing to support data centers. National Economic Council Director Kevin Hassett told CNBC on Friday that he expects data center growth to continue as forecast, despite Democrats' big election wins.

The big question now is how quickly AI spending will generate enough cash for hyperscalers to keep investors comfortable with future capex plans. For the neoclouds, any sign of consolidation or retrenchment in capex could result in fewer or shorter contracts for data center compute or delays in prepayments.

Goldman analysts said hyperscalers collectively reported negative free cash flow in the third quarter as capital spending exceeded operating cash flow.

"Delayed monetization and growth in purchase commitments—which we view as additional and sizable uses of cash, separate from Capex—are the key risks to more debt issuance, subject to market conditions," according to the bank.

Peak AI debt issuance may now be closer at hand. Bank of America analysts estimates that AI-related credit issuance will rise 31% from 2026 to 2027, hitting $685 billion, including public and private debt. They then see it falling 23% to $531 billion in 2028.

As for the risk? "Downside to these forecasts exists from a potential slowdown in AI infrastructure projects on the back of the Blue Wave, which will reduce capex needs and thus debt market funding," BofA said in a note.

-CNBC's Nick Wells and Michael Bloom contributed reporting

Mac lookalikes multiply as Linux raids Apple's wardrobe

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

New and revived desktops promise Cupertino polish, but our quick tests found a few seams showing

Linux can do many things, but good looks aren't always its strong point. Apple has rather more of a reputation in that department, and a crop of new projects is bringing Mac-style polish to the open source desktop.

Some are all-new, such as the Argentinian Lord OS, a whole new GUI desktop on top of Debian. Another, Cutefish, is a revival of a pre-ChatGPT project that never got as far as a complete release. Now it has version 1.0 for both x86-64 and Arm64, plus it's working on Mac binary compatibility via a work-in-progress layer called Reef. Ukrainian Desert OS is already at version 5.5 – practically a venerable elder in this company, despite appearing to be only about a year old.

Meanwhile, ClokeOS uses Xfce for its desktop, but underneath, the OS is based on Apple's FOSS XNU kernel. The project lead plans to offer compatibility with multiple types of binaries: Linux apps, plus Mac apps via Darling, and Windows apps via Wine.

This isn't new. Much as Cutefish has been revived, last year we reported on PearOS Linux, a very Mac-like skin on KDE Plasma. It has hooks in place for ambitious future features, such as Siri-style speech recognition.

What's striking is the number of projects appearing and the pace of their development. Some offer both x86-64 and Arm64 builds, with Raspberry Pi editions in certain cases. For human coders, this would be months of hard work, but if it's just checking an additional box in an LLM development tool, then why wouldn't you? It's almost no extra work, but it widens the potential audience.

Coveting Mac-like good looks is a longstanding urge among Linux users, and with legitimate reason. There's nothing wrong with Macs, even if you are mainly a FOSS user. Modern macOS, or OS X as it was called a decade ago, is a Unix underneath its shiny proprietary desktop, and many of the flagship Linux FOSS apps also have Mac versions, from Firefox and Thunderbird, via LibreOffice, to VirtualBox, plus less well-known but very handy apps such as Ferdium and Panwriter. We've written about the usefulness of FOSS apps on macOS before, including for reviving elderly Macs.

Just a couple of months ago, we published a two-part guide on how to give Xfce a macOS-like layout and then how to tweak it so apps fit in better. We focused on the functionality, though, not the looks – we don't really care much for such things. If it does matter to you, though, you're not alone, and there are lots of themes and skins to make Xfce look prettier.

Some of these are all-in-one packages that you can install on an existing distro to give it a new look. One recent example is Cupertino, developed with assistance from Claude. From a few years ago, there are Small Sur and Ventura-Xfce. From nine years ago, long pre-LLM, there is osXFCE.

We can see the appeal. From 2006 to 2020, Macs used Intel x86 processors, and those machines are now all falling off the end of Apple support – only four Intel models supported last year's macOS 26 "Tahoe," support for which is just starting to reach OpenCore Legacy Patcher. Earlier this year, The Reg FOSS desk upgraded his 11-year-old iMac to macOS 15 "Sequoia" with OCLP 2.5.1. The first experimental OCLP 3.0 release appeared last month, and it's now up to 3.0-rc5. This adds support for macOS 26, Apple's final major macOS release for Intel Macs. That puts a ceiling on future macOS upgrades for both home-built Hackintoshes and older Macs kept going with unofficial patches.

We gave LordOS a brief spin using its OVA virtual machine image. It installed and booted without trouble, and looks impressive. Last month, we wrote about ZacOS-9 and Mavernix, and similarly, we gave ZacOS-9 a very quick spin in a VM. The resemblance to our recent experience with ZacOS-9 went beyond appearances. In both, we found that the keyboard UI is largely missing: standard keystrokes didn't work. In LordOS, we found some menu options duplicated in two places in an un-Mac-like way, such as "About" options in both the app menu and the Help menu. The LordOS GitHub repository does not credit any LLM assistants, but it's gone though nine test releases in three weeks.

The inevitable end of life of x86 Macs is driving fresh interest in FOSS OSes. They offer a life extension for genuine Macs as well as the enticing prospect of Mac-like polish for existing FOSS OSes. Combine that with the fast, easy code generation offered by LLM coding assistants, and we think there's going to be a lot more of this. In 2025, we saw one macOS-like Linux project rebooted. This October, we saw two new ones. In November, we've found four more. We have a feeling that these are just the first trickles of what may soon become a flood. ®

The Simple Drink Swap Linked To 41% More Weight Loss

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Women who changed their usual lunchtime drink lost 41 per cent more weight over 18 months. What made the difference?

Women who changed their usual lunchtime drink lost 41 per cent more weight over 18 months. What made the difference?

Swapping diet drinks for water could help people lose more weight.

Women with type 2 diabetes who make the switch lose around 41 percent more weight over 18 months than those who continue drinking diet beverages.

They are also twice as likely to achieve diabetes remission during a structured weight-management programme.

The findings raise a question about whether a drink’s calorie count tells the whole story about its effects on weight.

Sweet taste without the accompanying energy might influence appetite signals.

The results suggest that everyday drink choices could play a useful supporting role in longer-term weight management.

The research involved around 80 women who were overweight or had obesity, had type 2 diabetes and regularly consumed diet drinks.

Researchers randomly assigned them either to replace their usual diet beverage with water after lunch five times a week or to continue drinking it.

Both groups took part in a six-month weight-loss programme followed by a year devoted to maintaining their progress.

By the end, women assigned to water had lost an average of 6.82 kg, compared with 4.85 kg among those continuing with diet drinks.

The difference amounted to almost 2 kg of additional weight loss across the programme.

The water group also showed greater improvements in measures including fasting blood glucose, insulin resistance and blood fats.

Diabetes remission was reported in 90 percent of women assigned to water, compared with 45 percent of those continuing diet beverages.

Dr Hamid R. Farshchi, study co-author, said:

“It’s a small change with the potential for a big impact on long-term health outcomes.”

The brain may respond differently when a sweet taste arrives without the energy normally supplied by sugar.

In a separate experiment involving around 75 adults, participants reported greater hunger after consuming sucralose than sugar, and the sweetener altered activity in the hypothalamus, a brain region involved in appetite regulation (Chakravartti et al., 2025).

However, hunger ratings after sucralose did not differ from those after water, so the findings do not establish that sweetened drinks necessarily make people hungrier than plain water.

The mismatch between sweetness and energy might help explain some appetite responses, but whether it contributed to the weight-loss difference remains unknown.

The study was published in the journal Diabetes (Farshchi Nasr et al., 2025).
