These Alignment Tips Are the Key to Balancing in Half Moon Pose

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

(Photo: Andrew Clark; Design in Canva by Laura Harold)

In Yoga Journal’s Archives series, we share a curated collection of articles originally published in past issues beginning in 1975. This article about how to do Half Moon Pose first appeared in the January-February 2003 issue of Yoga Journal. Find more of our Archives here.

Every yoga pose is but a wrapper for its unique internal gift. Ardha Chandrasana (Half Moon Pose) gives you the license to fly. With only one hand and one foot touching the ground, the rest of your body is free to soar. Your body stretches from head to heel, creating a sense of wide-dancing euphoria. Your heart center opens, expanding to embrace all the world. Among the standing poses, Ardha Chandrasana offers unique access to this vast, unbridled freedom of the heart.

Yet to achieve this unbounded freedom, we must first ground ourselves in a myriad of details. I am frequently asked, “Why are there so many details to attend to in each pose? Why should I make a conscious effort to straighten my legs, rotate my hips, or lift the inner arches of my feet in Ardha Chandrasana? Why not just switch off my brain, relax, and feel?”

I have found that attending to detail actually deepens the ability to feel, rather than hindering it, and makes it possible to receive the inner gifts of yoga. Though details can sometimes be overwhelming, attention to them refines your evolution and gives you great fulfillment and joy.

On the most basic level, details protect you during your practice. On another level, details ensure that you receive the benefits of the pose; detailed instructions help you bring the specific energetic gift of the pose into yourself. For energy to flow correctly, precise alignment is required. Without alignment, energy drains away and the pose collapses.

Of course, refinements can be carried too far, and you can become so focused on details that you lose sight of the large energy flows that circulate through the whole body. The general rule is that you need enough detail so that there will be no injury to the body *and* so that the pose serves its unique function. A useful approach, especially at first, is to work on just one or two details each time you practice the pose, gradually adding more subtle details after you attain the basics of the physical alignment. Trying to do subtle inner work before becoming physically aligned will lead to a collapse in the posture and a sense of frustration.

Details have more profound purposes as well. When you work on generalities, your mind is dull. When you start to focus on detail—in Ardha Chandrasana or in any pose—your awareness is refined. And awareness is the essence of yoga. Through the attention to detail in your practice, through the cultivation of refinement, and through the constant and ever more careful attention to your existence, you will slowly become aware of the soft, inner voice that will guide you to your spirit and to your fulfillment.

Supta Padangusthasana II (Reclining Hand-to-Big-Toe Pose, side variation) prepares you for Ardha Chandrasana, since it contains all the major movements of Ardha Chandrasana but does not require you to bear weight or to balance. Working on this pose will prevent strain in the buttock muscles as you later try to hold the leg up in Ardha Chandrasana.

To do Supta Padangusthasana II, lie on your back with both legs straight. Bend your right leg and catch your right big toe with the first two fingers of your right hand. (Use a belt around the instep of the foot if you cannot reach your toe.) Exhaling, slowly straighten your right leg completely, fully contracting the thigh muscles of both legs. You should maintain this quadriceps contraction throughout the pose. On your next exhalation, press your right sitting bone away from your head and bring your right leg out to the right side and down toward the ground; if possible, rest the outer right ankle on the floor.

Rather than letting your left hip rise, work to make both iliac crests (front hip bones) equidistant from the floor. This will create flexibility in the adductors and release tense muscles in the front of your pelvis so that when you do Ardha Chandrasana, the pelvis will rest evenly on the head of the standing-leg femur (thighbone). Press your left thigh into the floor. Placing your left hand on your left frontal pelvis, pull it to the left to help open it (Figure 1). Stay for three to nine deep breaths. Exhaling, bring your right leg up so that your arm is once again perpendicular to the floor. Release your right foot, bringing it back down beside the left. Repeat on the other side.

Yoga practitioners move into Ardha Chandrasana from Trikonasana (Figure 2) because the pelvis and the front-leg thighbone are in similar positions in both poses. If you can maintain their placement as you move into Ardha Chandrasana, the pose becomes much easier.

In Trikonasana, take a deep inhalation and visualize your body moving to the next step. Slowly bend your right knee, keeping the right hip externally rotated. Reach forward with your right hand and place the fingertips on the floor about one-and-a-half feet in front of your right foot and slightly to the little toe side (Figure 3). On an exhalation, shift more weight onto your right hand, lifting the left leg toward the ceiling until it becomes parallel to the floor. If you cannot lift your left leg directly from Trikonasana, slide your left foot closer to your right before coming up. Keep your left hand stable and reaching up toward the ceiling throughout your transition. This will greatly improve your balance.

As you move into Ardha Chandrasana, spread the toes and toe mounds of both feet as far apart as you can. This will energize your feet, bring vitality to your brain, and stabilize your movement. Straighten the right leg, powerfully rotating your hip externally so that the right kneecap continues to face your right toes. Even after your right knee is completely straight, continue to rotate the right hip externally. This is the most important action for maintaining safety in the pose, since it protects your inner knee ligaments, a place where many people have searing pain from hyperextension. This rotation also places the head of your femur accurately into joint so that when you lift the left side of your pelvis, you will place your weight evenly on the right hip joint.

Once in Ardha Chandrasana, distribute your weight evenly between the inner and outer foot of your supporting leg. You will notice that your weight tends to drop to your outer foot. To counteract this, consciously press the big toe mound and the inner heel into the floor while lifting your arch. This action will improve your sense of balance because your foot is the foundation of your pose; its lateral stability will determine the stability of your knee and hip.

If your right knee has rotated internally and you find it difficult to rotate it externally, bend your knee a bit. This action will release your hip and make rotation easier. If your standing-leg knee hyperextends, press the calf muscle forward toward your shin, microbend your knee, and press the top of your thigh (the upper quadriceps) back toward your hamstrings. Maintaining both these movements realigns your leg and prevents your knee joint from locking and distorting.

Now lift your left ilium (hip bone) toward the ceiling. Be careful not to press it back, for this will jam your sacroiliac joints and may eventually cause a compression injury. Another point of caution: Lifting your left ilium will tend to make your right leg rotate internally, and this must be resisted to prevent the damage to your inner knee ligaments. Your right leg’s external rotation must always take priority over the lift of your left ilium. When you do become able to lift your left ilium while maintaining the right hip’s external rotation, you will find that the weight of your body will rest on the center of the hip joint, thereby relieving tension in the outer muscles of your right leg, where many beginners get cramps. For additional stability of your right hip, hug your right outer thigh toward your inner thigh before lifting the left ilium.

The outer right ankle bone, the outer right knee, and the greater trochanter of the right leg (the bony knob at the top outer edge of the thigh) should be in one straight line perpendicular to the floor. If your standing leg is leaning toward your head, there will be compression in the rear of that hip joint and excessive weight in the hand and head. Alternately, if your standing leg is leaning toward your back leg, there will be compression in the front of your standing-leg hip joint and your back leg will then feel sluggish and heavy.

Your standing leg should also be perpendicular when viewed from the side. The right sitting bone tends to fall behind you, which collapses your right groin and puts tension in your outer right hip. Press the outer right hip forward until your right sitting bone is directly above your right heel. Simultaneously, lift your right outer ankle and outer leg toward your hip.

To add a more rigorous element of truth to the alignment of your legs, practice this pose with a wall behind you, placing the little toe of your right foot next to the wall and your right heel approximately a half-inch away from the wall. This makes the pose more difficult because your standing leg is now prevented from dropping backward. In this position, you must squeeze your outer right leg inward and lift your left pelvis straight up in order to do the pose.

Energize the muscles throughout both your legs, creating a sense of compactness, as if all the flesh of your legs is being drawn into your bones. This will create a feeling that your right leg has become a pillar supporting your body; it will also make your left leg feel much lighter.

Ardha Chandrasana not only strengthens the legs but also uses the standing leg to bring energy up from the earth. This upward drawing of energy is a foundational principle in yoga and in many martial arts; it is why you contract your quadriceps in the standing poses. When your quadriceps are relaxed, energy does not lift up your inner legs; when you pull the quadriceps up, they become “pumps” that bring the energy from the earth into your pelvis, where your root chakra and second chakra reside. These chakras cannot function well without a flow of energy. Ardha Chandrasana gives you an opportunity to create this flow.

There are other actions that assist this process of lifting energy. I have found that only through combining the lift of the arches and the lift of the quadriceps can the energy of the earth be transmitted into the pelvis and transmuted into corporeal energy. Lift the arch of your standing leg, suck energy up your inner leg through your groin, and feed the energy into the pelvis via a slight lift of your perineum (Mula Bandha). To accomplish these actions, begin by pressing down the mound of your big toe and the inner heel of your standing leg. To create stability, the outer edge of your standing foot also has to be pressed down strongly.

Thus, you will create arches both lengthwise and widthwise, between your big toe and heel as well as between the inner edge and outer edge of your foot.

Now turn your attention to the left leg. As you continue to rotate the right hip externally (so your knee points directly toward the middle of your foot), externally rotate your left leg so that your left kneecap faces front. Once your left kneecap is facing front, do not rotate your left hip any further.

The next action in your left leg should be to press the top of your thigh back toward your hamstrings; then lengthen the back of your leg into your heel. Simultaneously, draw the left side of the pit of your abdomen—the region of your belly just above your pubic bone—toward your head, creating a stretch across your left groin. This will prevent your left thighbone from falling forward and your left buttock from over-contracting.

To prevent fatigue in the left leg, press it down as if you were resting it on a big balloon. Instead of hanging the upper leg from the outer hip muscles, contract your inner thigh muscles toward your outer thigh. This will take the tension out of your outer hip muscles and strengthen your inner leg, including your adductors.

Two primary actions that are involved in Ardha Chandrasana are the “stacking” of the pelvis and the lifting of the pit of the abdomen. The stacking of your pelvis on your right hip joint not only ensures your safety but also aligns your pelvis to receive energy from your right leg. When the left side of your pelvis moves away from the right foot in Ardha Chandrasana, you will get the sense of your pelvis as an ever-widening bowl. This widening makes the pelvis available for the energy that rises from the earth through your standing leg.

Similarly, the lift of the pit of your abdomen aligns your pelvis with your rib cage, making the floor of your pelvis and your diaphragm parallel. This alignment charges the energy of your abdomen and nourishes your kidneys. As you draw the pit of your abdomen toward your head, your tailbone will move toward your perineum and your sitting bones will tuck toward your top leg heel. Do not tighten or flatten your stomach. Instead, lengthen from your pubis to your chest. This action realigns your pelvis and extends your lower back, preventing strain and cramping around the lumbar spine.

If you have stiff hamstrings or are perhaps new to Ardha Chandrasana, place a block underneath your right hand. This will increase your stability in the pose and take stress off your right leg, thereby allowing you to lift your left pelvis and pull the pit of your abdomen upward without distorting your spine.

As you continue to work in the pose, attempt to make your left kneecap, belly button, and sternum face front by spinning the left side of your chest and abdomen back. This action prevents the abdominal organs from falling into the front of your belly (their state in most daily activities) and makes them instead fall toward your right side. This massages your abdominal organs and relieves stress in your lower digestive system.

In Ardha Chandrasana, once you have filled your pelvis with life force from the earth, draw this energy up into the heart center for inner reflection and inner work. This movement of energy is one of the foundational elements in the way I teach standing poses.

When you do Ardha Chandrasana to your right, your tendency will be to make the right side of your torso shorter than your left side. Instead, work to keep both sides of the waist equally long. To lengthen your right side, press your right armpit forward, opening the right side of your chest by working your right kidney toward the front of your body. Drawing your outer right hip toward your left heel will also help balance the two sides of your waist and your spine.

In addition, press both shoulder blades toward the buttocks and away from each other. These actions release the congested sensation and cramping that can occur in the upper back behind the heart center. The shoulder blade actions also free your shoulders and neck so that your head can turn without tension.

Try to bring both arms in one straight line perpendicular to the floor. To do this, press your left shoulder blade away from your head more strongly than the right. Also, you should counteract the tendency of the upper arm to drop behind you as you open and turn your chest to face forward. Restrict this tendency by lifting the back of your arm up through the nails of your fingers. The upper arm should now be perpendicular to the floor as viewed from the front and side of the room. Lifting your back arm will stabilize it and prevent it from falling backward. The more stable your upper arm, the more stable your pose.

Your bottom arm, like your bottom leg, lifts energy from the earth into your heart. Placing the pads of your fingertips on the floor, form a cup with the right hand. Suck energy up from the earth through this cup into your chest.

All through out your practice of Ardha Chandrasana, always keep your upper-arm palm wide and facing forward, with your fingertips and thumb tip pointing straight up toward the ceiling and with your fingers and thumb pressing together. These actions align your hand energy and develop concentration. You should pull equally into your thumb and little finger to keep both sides of your wrist even.

Do not let your body fall into a backbend; doing a backbend in this pose dissipates the energy from your kidneys through your belly and chest. In contrast, moving the body into one plane aligns the internal flows of spinal energy from your perineum to the crown of your head. When carefully combined, the following three actions will prevent your body from doing a backbend: Stretch from your left sacrum and right sitting bone into your left heel, from the pit of your abdomen into your diaphragm, and from your kidneys into your front upper chest. These stretches will additionally help move your spine toward your head, while pulling your coccyx and left leg toward your left heel.

Once you have incorporated all of these details into Ardha Chandrasana I (see Figure 4), your brain will have given out all the instructions necessary to create alignment in your physical body. After you reach and hold the alignment, your brain’s job is over. Next, you will have to separate your consciousness from your brain, moving it out of your head and into your heart while you perform the internal actions of the pose. To facilitate this separation of consciousness from your brain, relax the muscles of your face, jaw, and tongue.

As you inhale, bring vitality from your lungs into your whole body; as you exhale, make any necessary adjustments to your alignment. You will find that inhalations draw life force into your body, whereas exhalations channel that force through your body. Keep your breath soft and long, breathing only through your nose with the softness of Ujjayi Pranayama (Victorious Breath): Drawing your breath audibly across the back of your throat, inhale with a slight “s” sound and exhale with an “h.” All these actions will help your breath connect your brain to your heart and the world outside you with the world within.

The Cybertruck is dead – what went wrong?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Elon Musk is winding down production, and I won’t be shedding a tear.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Tesla is winding down Cybertruck production. Elon Musk has confirmed that manufacturing at the Texas Gigafactory will scale back around mid-2027 to make room for more Cybercab robotaxis. And so ends one of the strangest design experiments in automotive history, one riddled with ugly mistakes and design fails.

Okay, ‘ends’ might be jumping the gun a little. Elon Musk hasn’t said outright that the Cybertruck is being discontinued, and it’s not clear whether production could move elsewhere. But as posted on X, Texas is the only known facility equipped to build its rather specialised parts, so the writing is pretty clearly on the wall.

Tesla will be winding down production of the Cybertruck at Gigafactory Texas around the middle of next year to make room for increased Cybercab productionNovember 6, 2026

It was always going to be a tough sell. When Musk unveiled the Cybertruck in 2019, he promised a revolution in pickup design. What we got looked like it had rolled straight out of a dystopian sci-fi movie: angular stainless steel, sharp edges and a comically oversized windscreen with one comically oversized, slow wiper. Different? Absolutely. I’ll give it that.

The reaction was instant, with designers and the internet having a field day. We covered the fallout in our 2019 look at the designers taking aim at Tesla’s Cybertruck, and the argument over whether it’s genius or a low-poly disaster has never really gone away.

The launch event set the tone for everything that followed. Tesla’s chief designer, Franz von Holzhausen, took a metal ball to the supposedly unbreakable windows, and they promptly shattered. Not exactly the show of strength Tesla was after, and a pretty cringe bit of live theatre for a truck sold on its toughness.

Then came the production delays, shifting specs, and a price that jumped from the original promises. Musk once talked about selling 250,000 Cybertrucks a year. According to Kelley Blue Book figures reported by Reuters, Tesla sold just 20,237 in the US during 2025, down 48% from the year before. Yikes.

We’ve been tracking the Cybertruck’s design misadventures since that first reveal, and it constantly surprised us for all the wrong reasons. We looked at early Cybertruck sketches that show how different the finished design could have been, and even imagined what happens when other carmakers go angular in our look at what every car would look like as a Cybertruck. The Mini Cooper, the first car I ever owned, in particular, wasn’t the looker it used to be.

Sign up to Creative Bloq's daily newsletter, which brings you the latest news and inspiration from the worlds of art, design and technology.

But the issues ran deeper than looks. In June 2023, we reported on leaked design and engineering flaws that made the Cybertruck sound even more terrifying, including problems with braking, handling, noise and water leaks. Then there’s the branding. In April 2024, we rounded up the six worst Cybertruck design fails, from that questionable logo to the accelerator pedal recall.

Then came the reports of bits falling off and the off-road truck getting stuck in mud – are you getting the picture? In March 2025, another Cybertruck design disaster emerged involving exterior panels coming loose. It’s quite something when a truck styled like an armoured tank can’t keep its own bodywork on.

To be fair, the Cybertruck was never meant to be universally loved, and ambition may have overtaken the practicalities of mass production. Musk wanted a vehicle that looked like nothing else on the road, and he got it. But good product design has to do more than turn heads. It has to work, make sense for the people buying it, and survive the rather less glamorous reality of everyday use, whether it's offroading or just driving to the shops without a bumper falling off. The Cybertruck struggled on all three counts.

That heavy, angular build also proved a barrier in Europe, where it failed to meet pedestrian-safety requirements. And the stainless-steel exterior may look suitably apocalyptic, but it brought its own manufacturing and finishing headaches.

Then there’s the branding. That huge angular silhouette was already shouting loud enough, and the aggressive typography and Cybertruck identity just pushed the whole thing further into concept-car territory. I love seeing designers take a big swing at something genuinely unusual. But there’s bold, and then there’s a design that seems to have skipped the bit where someone asks, ‘Is this actually a good idea?’

For contrast, check out some of the best car logos on the road today. Ferrari, Porsche and Mercedes have built enduring identities for a reason: they tell you something about the product and its heritage without yelling at you.

Tesla is now turning its attention to the Cybercab, its two-seat autonomous taxi, with the Texas factory being repurposed to ramp up production. The company is also pushing harder into AI and robotics, so the Cybertruck’s exit is part of a much bigger change of direction.

We’ve already had a glimpse of what that next chapter might look like, and Tesla’s Cybercab has its own rather silly design fail. Maybe the Cybertruck’s lessons haven’t quite sunk in yet.

So here’s to the end of the Cybertruck era, one that made us smile more than it had drivers, and to whatever the Cybercab era has in store.

Ian Dean is Editor, Digital Arts & 3D at Creative Bloq, and the former editor of many leading magazines. These titles included ImagineFX, 3D World and video game titles Play and Official PlayStation Magazine. Ian launched Xbox magazine X360 and edited PlayStation World. For Creative Bloq, Ian combines his experiences to bring the latest news on digital art, VFX and video games and tech, and in his spare time he doodles in Procreate, ArtRage, and Rebelle while finding time to play Xbox and PS5.

Perfectly preserved owl fossil from 47 million years ago could help to solve a major evolutionary mystery

A stunning fossil owl from Germany that has its last meal preserved in its stomach helped researchers to narrow down the time when these birds mostly became active at night.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A fledgling owl that lived 47 million years ago in what is now Germany offers tantalizing new clues about when most of these birds became nocturnal, a new study shows. The discovery brings researchers one step closer to finally solving the biggest mystery about these birds.

The young owl is a newly described species named Masillastrix blattophaga. Researchers unearthed the almost perfectly preserved bird in 2025 in the Messel Pit, an ancient lake surrounded by a forest that is now the richest fossil site in the world for understanding life in the Eocene epoch (56 million to 34 million years ago). The skull is missing, but the rest of the fossil was so well preserved that researchers found the owl's last meal in its stomach: a greenish-blue, iridescent cockroach.

The cockroach is one of several pieces of evidence suggesting that this tiny owl was diurnal, or active primarily during the day. Iridescence is associated with a diurnal lifestyle, so it appears that the owl, which measured less than 5 inches (12 centimeters) long, was hunting in the daytime, researchers said in the new study.

This indicates that owls were not nocturnal 47 million years ago and so must have switched to the night-owl lifestyle more recently, said study first author Gerald Mayr, a paleontologist and head of ornithology at the Senckenberg Research Institute and the Natural History Museum Frankfurt in Germany.

"There exists a consensus that owls must have evolved from a diurnal ancestor," Mayr told Live Science in an email. That's because some early owl fossils have head-rotation structures similar to those of diurnal raptors and bony ridges above the eye sockets that could have shaded the owls' eyes from the sun.

Therefore, it is plausible that the Messel owl was fully diurnal, rather than active during the daytime and nighttime as some owls are today, because that would imply that some of its ancestors had already evolved nocturnal traits, Mayr said.

Researchers also found a cockroach identical to the one in the Messel owl's stomach in sediments just a few feet away from the new fossil. This well-preserved cockroach helped the team to identify the insect inside the owl, the researchers wrote in the study, which was published Nov. 2 in the journal Current Biology.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

It's possible that the owl ate the cockroach at dawn or at dusk, which might cast doubt on whether the bird was diurnal. But there are other clues hinting that the owl was diurnal, including the appearance of some of the owl's feathers and the stoutness of its upper wing bones.

Mayr said he and his colleagues believe the shape of the upper wing bones, or humeri, is an indicator of when owls are active. The Messel owl's stocky humeri hint that the species flew during the day, when silent movement doesn't confer a significant advantage because there is enough light to see predators. Conversely, modern nocturnal owls have slim humeri and fly almost soundlessly, he said.

The fossil was so well preserved that researchers identified a cockroach in the stomach that was likely the owl's last meal.

Assuming this hypothesis is correct, the earliest known nocturnal owl species are from about 35 million years ago, in the Late Eocene, Mayr said. Owl fossils from this period and more recently look a lot like modern owls and have elongated humeri, he noted. The oldest known owl in the fossil record, Ogygoptynx wetmorei, dates to around 60 million years ago.

This means that owls likely became nocturnal between 47 million and 35 million years ago. This is the best current estimate, because the owl fossil record is very sparse and species are often described from only a few isolated bones, according to the study.

"We still know so little about the early evolution of modern birds," Mayr said, adding that every fossil discovery has the power to change even well-established ideas in the field.

The big question that remains is why owls became nocturnal. Previous theories suggested that owls evolved this behavior to avoid competition with diurnal birds of prey or to benefit from a proliferation of nocturnal mammals. But studies show that both of these groups exploded much later than 35 million years ago — around 20 million to 25 million years ago — suggesting there wasn't a clear advantage driving owls to switch to nocturnal lifestyles at that time.

The new results have deepened the uncertainty of why owls turned nocturnal. But "by narrowing down the time frame when owls became nocturnal, our study may eventually contribute to solving this mystery," Mayr said.

Mayr, G., Šmídová, L., Richter, A., & Wedmann, S. (2026). Evidence for diurnal habits in an exceptionally preserved Eocene owl. Current Biology. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cub.2026.09.079

Sascha is a U.K.-based staff writer at Live Science. She holds a bachelor’s degree in biology from the University of Southampton in England and a master’s degree in science communication from Imperial College London. Her work has appeared in The Guardian and the health website Zoe. Besides writing, she enjoys playing tennis, bread-making and browsing second-hand shops for hidden gems.

About half of people with long COVID see meaningful improvement in their symptoms after three years, a new study found.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Symptoms of long COVID ease in half of patients within three years of the infection that kicked off their illness.

That's the primary conclusion of a new study that tracked over 1,000 patients with long COVID, a chronic condition that can emerge after an infection with the SARS-CoV-2 virus. Among the patients, the chance of improvement varied sharply depending on how the condition affected their bodily systems.

In the study, published Tuesday (Nov. 3) in the journal Annals of Internal Medicine, roughly 60% of patients whose predominant symptoms were changes to their ability to taste or smell had significantly improved three years after their first positive test. But less than 20% of those with a multisystem presentation had improved in that time frame.

This latter group had symptoms that affected their cardiac, nervous and gastrointestinal systems, among others. An expert told Live Science that the condition resembled myalgic encephalomyelitis/chronic fatigue syndrome (ME/CFS), which often arises after an infection and similarly impacts a wide range of bodily systems.

The new study's findings could help direct the structure of future clinical trials of treatments for long COVID, experts said.

The patients in the study were enrolled in the long-term RECOVER Adult Study. Funded by the National Institutes of Health, the project has recruited thousands of adults across the United States who previously tested positive for COVID-19.

Researchers scored patients based on how they were affected by a range of symptoms, including fatigue, brain fog, shortness of breath and heart palpitations. The study included patients who scored more than 10 points on a 30-point scale — called the 2024 LC Research Index (LCRI) — which attempted to capture these different symptom combinations.

Get the world’s most fascinating discoveries delivered straight to your inbox.

Nearly 1,020 patients met this requirement and were tracked over time; they had in-person appointments and filled out symptom surveys every three months. Scientists marked patients as "improved" once they recorded LCRI scores of 10 or less at two consecutive visits. They found that 36% of the participants had improved two years after their inciting COVID-19 infection, and that figure rose to 50% after three years.

LCRIs hit zero for 26% of the participants after three years, meaning all of their symptoms were gone at the time of the survey.

Reeder said he was careful to avoid describing the participants as "recovered." This decision was influenced by input from the long COVID community to the RECOVER Adult Study, he said. They highlighted that their symptoms can wax and wane, whereas "recovery" implies a permanent resolution. The new study didn't capture, for example, whether individual patients who recorded meaningful improvement later relapsed.

The researchers analyzed which SARS-CoV-2 strain was associated with the most long COVID cases, controlling for patient vaccination status.

Some patients' journeys were far less straightforward than the top-line data might suggest. Although men and women improved at similar rates, adults under 46 were more likely to improve than older participants were. Patients affected by the Omicron variants of SARS-CoV-2, which emerged in 2021 and quickly became the dominant strain of the virus, were more likely to improve than those infected with earlier strains.

Some of the starkest differences in improvement rates were seen between patients with different symptoms.

In a previous study, RECOVER researchers had grouped participants into five subtypes, using an algorithm to connect patients with similar predominant symptoms:

In the new study, patients in the last three groups were less likely to get better within three years. The final group's rate was particularly low, at just 19%.

Dr. Carmen Scheibenbogen, an immunologist at Charité University Medicine Berlin, said these patients' progress resembles that of people with ME/CFS.

"It looks as if at least a major subgroup is suffering from ME-CFS," Scheibenbogen said, noting that COVID-19 may have triggered their condition. "This is a disease which is triggered by different infections, but in the end, the disease [itself] is very similar," she said.

Like long COVID, ME/CFS can affect a wide array of bodily systems, and it remains poorly understood despite recent advances in research.

The team's new findings highlight the need for clinical trials to test possible treatments for long COVID. Both Scheibenbogen and study co-author Harrison Reeder, a biostatistician at Massachusetts General Hospital, said these subtypes would be important to consider in the design of these potential trials.

The multisystem subgroup, Reeder said, "might be a group where an effective intervention can make a bigger difference than people who stand to potentially have a higher chance of improvement regardless."

Future work would need to further scientists' understanding of people affected by long COVID at the level of their genes and proteins, both scientists said.

"There's a lot of work to do in RECOVER and in the world of long COVID research," Harrison said. "There's a lot of opportunity."

This article is for informational purposes only and is not meant to offer medical advice.

Reeder, H. T et al (2026). Timing of Long COVID Improvement in Prospectively Followed RECOVER-Adult Participants. Annals of Internal Medicine. https://doi.org/10.7326/ANNALS-25-05275

He has reported on everything from brain-interface technology to shape-shifting materials science, and from the rise of predatory conferencing to the importance of newborn-screening programs. He is a former staff writer of Technology Networks.

Former Newcastle United attacker Allan Saint-Maximin isn't short of cash after a spell in Saudi Arabia

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Modern footballers keep their cards close to their chest when it comes to their personal lives, and most certainly don't tend to disclose how much they earn.

Former Newcastle United winger Allan Saint-Maximin, however, isn't your average footballer, though.

While footballers' wages are a constant topic of debate, leaked figures are routinely downplayed, and players almost never volunteer information on the exact sums to the general public.

In a candid social media post, the French forward revealed that he has pocketed an astonishing €45 million (£37.5m) net over three years in Saudi Arabia.

The admission arrived as Saint-Maximin sought to explain why he rejected an alleged €26m (£21.7m) takeover bid to buy out his custom-designed board game project.

Addressing his followers, the 29-year-old explained that he turned down the buyout offer because he prefers investment in the enterprise instead of surrendering control. He says he is determined to use the venture to create jobs and host prize-giving tournaments for underprivileged communities.

"Rest assured, I've already earned 45 million net in three years in Saudi Arabia, so it's not a 26 million offer that will shake me," Saint-Maximin wrote. "A billion, maybe we will discuss it, but we are still far from it! 26 million is a good deal. But changing people's lives is priceless."

The best features, fun and footballing quizzes, straight to your inbox every week.

Saint-Maximin remains one of the more beloved cult figures on Tyneside in recent memory. Signed from Nice in 2019, the winger was often the solitary bright spark during the bleak final years of Mike Ashley's ownership and Steve Bruce’s pragmatic managerial tenure.

With trademark designer headbands, electric dribbling, and social media rapport with supporters, Saint-Maximin often carried the attack and offered fans a semblance of escapism before the club's transformative 2021 takeover.

Saint-Maximin was sold to Saudi Pro League side Al-Ahli for around £23m in 2023, a deal that prompted scrutiny from rival Premier League clubs given that both Newcastle and Al-Ahli share majority ownership under Saudi Arabia’s Public Investment Fund (PIF).

After spending his debut campaign in Jeddah alongside Riyad Mahrez and Roberto Firmino, the Frenchman went on loan to Turkish giants Fenerbahce, linking up with Jose Mourinho.

Saint-Maximin then joined Mexican side Club America in 2025 but did not settle and returned to Europe, turning out for Racing Club de Lens for a time. Before long, though, he was on the move once more, joining MLS outfit Charlotte FC for whom he currently plays.

His board game enterprise, Helios, which he claims he spent years designing and testing, has already seen him hand out five-figure sums in prize money to amateur players.

Joe joined FourFourTwo as senior digital writer in July 2025 after five years covering Leeds United in the Championship and Premier League. Joe's 'Mastermind' specialist subject is 2000s-era Newcastle United having had a season ticket at St. James' Park for 10 years before relocating to Leeds and later London. Joe takes a keen interest in youth football, covering PL2, U21 Euros, as well as U20 and U17 World Cups in the past, in addition to hosting the industry-leading football recruitment-focused SCOUTED podcast. He is also one of the lucky few to have 'hit top bins' as a contestant on Soccer AM. It wasn't a shin-roller.

Oak Ridge labs gets hybrid quantum-HPC system to test qubit error correction

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Co-design platform for quantum error correction melds IBM quantum kit with AMD CPUs and GPUs

Oak Ridge National Laboratory (ORNL) now boasts a system for research into quantum error correction, combining IBM quantum processors with a high-performance compute (HPC) system based on AMD silicon.

The Quantum Science Center (QSC) at ORNL, funded by the US Department of Energy (DoE), unveiled what it calls a co-design platform for quantum error correction, marrying up quantum and HPC technology.

While useful, fault-tolerant quantum systems always seem to be at least a decade away, there have been notable advances over the past several years in the field of quantum error correction, according to ORNL. These include early demonstrations of protecting quantum information from environmental noise, which can cause loss of the fragile quantum states needed for computations.

"Solving the challenges of quantum error correction is essential to making quantum computing a practical tool for scientific discovery, which is a priority for Oak Ridge National Laboratory," said QSC director Travis Humble.

IBM Fellow and director of IBM Research Jay Gambetta said that it was no longer a question of whether error correction works, but how to accelerate and optimize error correction to build systems that scientists can use to tackle challenging problems.

"This new co-design platform for quantum error correction allows us to evaluate different approaches and determine the architectures, software and workflows that will scale," he stated.

ORNL's new tool combines an IBM Quantum System Two, equipped with the latest 120-qubit Nighthawk r2 quantum processors, and Umbra, a classical HPC system powered by AMD 5th Gen Epyc CPUs and Instinct MI355X GPU accelerators.

According to AMD, the CPUs provide the orchestration and general-purpose performance needed to coordinate everything, while the GPUs accelerate simulation, optimization and the parallel scientific computations surrounding quantum workloads.

Also part of the system are AMD Versal SoCs to provide the low-latency control, readout and decoding capabilities for the quantum circuitry.

ORNL says that this brings together the computing infrastructure needed for quantum error correction in a single environment, and opens it up to more researchers than ever before. They will use it to develop and validate quantum algorithms, workflows, and applications in an environment built for quantum-centric supercomputing, an emerging paradigm where quantum processors (QPUs), CPUs, and GPUs work together as one system.

IBM says that one promising approach the co-design platform cloud explore is hierarchical error correction. It describes this as using an inner code to protect qubits within a module, while an outer code wraps around the inner code’s logical qubits and adds a second layer of protection.

This approach could reach a trillion logical operations at a physical error rate of one in a thousand, IBM research suggested. These hierarchical codes rely on having powerful classical compute, and the co-design platform will give researchers a place to test and develop those ideas with GPUs in a low-latency environment, the firm states.

The co-design platform is likely to play a key part in the DoE’s Quantum Genesis Mission objectives. These include the recently announced Q Competition, an initiative to deliver a system with 100 logical qubits capable of performing hundreds of millions of fault-tolerant operations. It follows an executive order from President Trump to develop a full-blown quantum system by 2028.®

25 Best Gifts for New Homeowners (That They Will Actually Use)

We may earn revenue from the products available on this page and participate in affiliate programs. Learn More ›

Homeownership comes with its fair share of excitement, to-do lists, and maintenance tasks, and gifts that help new homeowners tackle projects are always appreciated. Although the following list of gift ideas contains a few cozy essentials and bits of personalized decor (you can find even more in our housewarming gifts guide), it focuses primarily on options to help start the homeownership journey off right. From practical air purifiers to reliable folding ladders, it includes top-rated home products across a variety of categories, many of them tested by our expert team.

New homeowners who don’t have a tool collection will appreciate the Stanley 94-248 65-Piece Homeowner’s Tool Kit for their homeowners’ survival kit, while those with a basic upright vacuum might appreciate adding the upgraded BLACK+DECKER dustbuster® reveal™ Handheld Pet to their supply closets. But the list doesn’t end there: Keep reading for more of the best gifts for new homeowners.

Whether human- or pet-caused, cleanups around the house are quick and easy with the BLACK+DECKER dustbuster® reveal™ Handheld Pet Vacuum. Engineered specifically to tackle stubborn pet hair, dirt, and cat litter, this cordless hand vac features specialized rubber tines that pull embedded fur from upholstery, carpets, and car seats. Its built-in extendable crevice tool and lightweight frame simplify maneuvering in tight spaces, and the transparent dust bin opens with the touch of a button so you don’t have to touch any debris.

Get the Dustbuster cordless hand vacuum at BLACK+DECKER or Amazon.

Sprucing up a new home for the holidays is always a memorable experience, and the Home Decorators Collection Balsam Fir pre-lit garland adds an extra-special touch. Measuring 12 feet long, this lush green garland features color-changing micro fairy LEDs designed to stay illuminated even if a bulb burns out, and thanks to its companion app, it’s easy to set schedules, switch between 10 dynamic light functions, and adjust brightness. Rated for both indoor and outdoor use, you can move it around every year to keep customizing your look.

Get the Home Decorators Collection garland at The Home Depot.

A new home comes with a new set of what-ifs, including the first power outage. The Bluetti Elite 10 Mini, one of our 2026 Gifts of the Year and the best ultra-portable pick in our Bluetti power stations guide, keeps phones, laptops, and other small electronics charged through its AC outlets and USB ports. At just under 4 pounds, it’s easy to stash in a hall closet or emergency kit until it’s needed. It isn’t built to run a refrigerator or space heater, but as a starter piece for keeping everyone connected until the lights come back on, it’s a thoughtful pick.

What our tester says: “At just under 4 pounds, the Elite 10 Mini is incredibly easy to carry around or toss into a bag without thinking twice.” —Paul Rankin, Product Reviews tester and writer

Get the Bluetti portable power station (various colors) at Bluetti, The Home Depot, or Amazon.

Until a new homeowner builds out a full tool collection, the Leatherman Wave Plus can cover a surprising number of small fixes. A new home means more square footage to keep clean, and the Roborock Qrevo S Pro takes vacuuming and mopping off a busy new homeowner’s to-do list. Its 18,500 Pa of suction lifts dirt and debris from both hard floors and carpet, while dual spinning mops scrub hard floors and automatically lift when the robot rolls onto a rug. The dock does most of the upkeep, too: It empties the dustbin for up to 65 days at a time, refills the water tank, washes the mops with hot water, and dries them with warm air to keep odors away. An anti-tangle side brush and all-rubber main brush cut down on hair wraps, and obstacle avoidance helps it steer around clutter on the floor.

Sertão Block and Amparo House / Asa Arquitetos

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Read the original version in Portuguese here.

Text description provided by the architects. Located at Haras Amparo Medeiros, in the rural area of Princesa Isabel in the Paraíba hinterland (sertão), Casa Amparo was designed based on an analysis of the local natural conditions and landscape. The rugged topography, characterized by rocky outcrops, resilient vegetation, and open views of the territory, guided a layout that seeks to accommodate the architecture to the different levels, preserving the natural features of the site.

Text description provided by the architects. This compensation may influence how and where offers appear on the site, including the order in which they appear on category pages. It does not influence our evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We do not include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

From the year you turn 50, catch-up rules let you add $8,000 a year to a 401(k) and $1,100 to an IRA beyond the regular limits. Raise your contributions to use them. Shift gradually toward bonds until a few years of retirement withdrawals sit in bonds and cash, so a crash can’t force you to sell stocks low.

Your 50s are the last decade when extra savings still have years to grow, and the first when a crash could hit just before you need the money.

The IRS’s catch-up rules stack the 401(k) catch-up on top of the regular limit of $24,500 a year. In the years you turn 60 through 63, the catch-up rises to $11,250 if your plan offers the higher amount. The IRA catch-up adds to the regular $7,500 limit.

At a hypothetical 6% annual return, an extra $8,000 a year in a 401(k) for the 10 years from age 55 to 65 grows to about $105,000.

To get the full catch-up, set your contribution rate to cover the regular limit plus the catch-up over the year. If your wages from your employer topped $150,000 last year, the catch-up has to go in as Roth money, taxed now instead of when you withdraw it. In that case, a plan with no Roth option can’t accept your catch-up at all.

Build the bond cushion gradually. Each year, move a little more of your savings from stocks to bonds, or send new contributions to bonds, rather than switching all at once. By your retirement date, aim to hold a few years of withdrawals in bonds and cash, since losses early in retirement do the most damage.

Start planning now, too, for when to claim Social Security and how you’ll pay for health insurance if you retire before Medicare begins at 65.

Raise your 401(k) contribution rate on your plan’s website before your next payday, since catch-ups come only out of paychecks and must go in before the plan year ends.

DarkSword iPhone Exploit Drains $4M in Crypto Theft

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Home » Crypto Market News » DarkSword iPhone Exploit Drains $4M in Crypto Theft

Zero-click browser vulnerability drains millions from unpatched devices by targeting stored recovery phrases.

A silent zero-click exploit chain targeting iPhones has drained more than $4 million in cryptocurrency across 365 TRON wallets, according to a report by Bitquery.

Dubbed DarkSword, the sophisticated attack kit compromises unpatched iOS devices through Safari—allowing hackers to extract private keys and seed phrases without requiring a single transaction signature or app download.

DarkSword, a kit for hacking iPhones, chains six iOS vulnerabilities to hijack devices running iOS 18.4 through 18.7. It compromises vulnerable iPhones through malicious websites opened in Apple’s Safari browser.

The exploit chain combines multiple security vulnerabilities to gain access to sensitive device information without requiring users to download an application or authorize a wallet transaction.

Google’s Threat Intelligence Group (GTIG), together with cybersecurity firms Lookout and iVerify, published technical analyses of the exploit on March 18, 2026. Their findings describe an attack chain capable of accessing passwords, messages, and cryptocurrency wallet information stored in Apple’s Keychain.

A separate investigation by cybersecurity firm Censys identified modules targeting 18 cryptocurrency wallet applications. The toolkit can reportedly search photos and note-taking applications for recovery phrases, potentially exposing wallets whose credentials were stored on the device.

Targeted applications reportedly include MetaMask, Phantom, Trust Wallet, and Coinbase. The vulnerability lies in the iPhone’s operating system rather than the individual wallet applications.

Unlike conventional phishing attacks, DarkSword does not necessarily require victims to connect their wallets or approve suspicious transactions. Attackers who obtain a private key or recovery phrase can use another device to sign and broadcast transactions.

Bitquery’s investigation identified a network of 43 collection addresses connected through a shared fee wallet. The addresses received funds from 365 TRON wallets between October 5 and November 4.

The investigation found that at least $4.06 million in USDT was transferred. Of the 365 wallets affected, the median loss was $1,029. Eight wallets lost more than $100,000 each, accounting for 63% of the total USDT losses. The attackers reportedly sent small amounts of TRX to victims’ wallets shortly before draining their balances.

Bitquery traced the transfers back to an initial transaction whose amount and timing matched those of a publicly reported iPhone theft. However, blockchain records cannot reveal which devices or wallet applications individual victims were using.

Apple has released security updates addressing the vulnerabilities used by DarkSword. Google recommends installing the latest available iOS version or enabling Apple’s Lockdown Mode when an update is not possible.

Changing wallet passwords or revoking token approvals is not sufficient if attackers already possess the recovery phrase. A compromised seed requires generating a new one and transferring the assets.

DarkSword device-level attacks bypass wallet approval checks, so exposed seed phrases can lead to total loss without a single signature from the victim.

Xbox's New PC App Update Is Impressive, Even If There's A Few Teething Problems

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Just yesterday, Microsoft began rolling out an update for the Xbox PC app, making it faster and more responsive on Windows desktop and handheld. We've had a little play with the new version, and our first impressions are mostly positive.

Xbox boss Asha Sharma posted a short video showing off the boot speed of the revamped Xbox PC app. It took a mere 5.2 seconds to load into the home page, clocking in a full 10 seconds quicker than the current version.

Having tried the new Xbox PC app ourselves, we can confirm that it's noticeably faster than the current version. It opens wonderfully swift on my Windows 10 desktop, stocked with 64GB of Sam Altman's precious FU-RAM, but it's also pretty nippy on my first-gen ASUS ROG Ally.

It was important to me that this new version worked well on my ROG Ally, because the current one takes an age to boot in the handheld's 15W Windows mode. I've never enjoyed that feeling of being forced to switch the ROG Ally into Performance or Turbo mode just to get the Xbox PC app running. Now, I don't have to. Big W for this update!

Beyond the speed improvements, the new app looks so much cleaner and feels much more in line with the Xbox console experience, which has been something I've always hoped we'd see: a kinda merger between the PC and Xbox console interfaces, with the latter serving as more of a base.

The side bar is much slicker too. Social stuff like who's online and what they're up to now display alongside Home, Library, Game Pass, and Store options. Cloud Gaming has been removed from the left-hand bar though, which made it a little awkward to access.

I found launching a game via the cloud a bit confusing. Almost like it's been hidden on purpose. I expected to be able to head to a cloud-enabled title, open its game page, and find the button to launch it via the cloud. Just like before. However, there's nothing to select. It simply says "I own this". Instead, I had to head back to the library page, hit Select, open the submenu, and choose "Play on Cloud". It's handy, but took a little searching to get working.

Remote Play has also been tucked away in a dropdown menu for some reason. I'd much rather see it on the left, alongside the other main tab options. I reckon with a bit more time the new Xbox PC app will be fantastic, and with a couple of updates it should work out some of the kinks I mentioned above.

So far I think the app update is really slick, very fast, and a much nicer experience than the current version. It's a good step in the right direction, and that seems to be the general consensus among Xbox users online, too:

During the next international break, AC Milan will see Koni De Winter at Milanello, with him rejecting a call-up.

De Winter has regularly been part of the Belgium set-up in recent years; however, his minutes have been few and far between, or at least they have recently. Before the World Cup, he was regularly getting minutes.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

However, his last showing for the national team came in June, when he played just 23 minutes. Since then, he’s only been seen on the bench, even after being called up in the most recent international break.

He’s still clearly part of the plans for the Red Devils, and Mark van Bommel revealed that he was offered a place in the most recent international squad. Yet, the defender has rejected his place, as the former Milan man told HLN (via MilanNews).

“He made the decision himself. I spoke to him and explained the situation to him. As a coach, as a former player, and as a father. In the end, he made this decision, and I can’t do anything about it. It’s not because he didn’t play last time.

“You have to accept it, but there will also be long-term consequences. He’s 24, so it’s a decision he made very early.”

Considering his lack of minutes and the birth of his child, it’s understandable why he’s said no. The threat of being punished for saying no seems harsh in this instance.

This Public-Land Virginia Hunter Chased the Same Massive Buck for 4 Years

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Published Nov 6, 2026 4:59 PM EST

Get everything from whitetail news and opinion pieces, to advanced hunting tactics and cutting-edge gear reviews.

Over many days of hunting a public mountain in Virginia this year Nick Dean had only seen three deer. But one of them was a giant whitetail buck he’d played cat-and-mouse with for multiple seasons.

“I had some trail camera photos of him, and on October 27 I had the buck at 40 yards, but it was too thick to shoot,” Dean tells Outdoor Life. “He was following a doe, and I just couldn’t slip an arrow through the cover to take him with my crossbow – so I passed the shot.”

Dean was understandably frustrated that he’d missed his chance at the buck he’d been after for four years. The public hunting area is steep, rocky, difficult to access, and there are not as many deer compared to other areas of Virginia.

“But in clubs I hunt I can only take one buck, and the challenge of taking a mountain buck is hard to resist,” says Dean, who works for an excavating company. “I like hunting the mountains and I’ve been doing it there for 20 years. While there are not as many deer as some other places in the state, there are some big bucks.”

Dean had another chance to take the giant buck a couple years ago during the muzzleloader season. But his black-powder rifle malfunctioned and misfired — again failing to capitalize on a close call with the buck.

But on Oct. 28, the afternoon after he saw the buck this year, Dean climbed 2.5 miles up the mountain once again to try for the whitetail. He decided to climb a tree at a different location on the trail where he’d seen the buck the prior day.

“It was about mid-afternoon when I got where I wanted to hunt, because I had to work that morning,” he says. “I found a couple trails about 100 yards from where I’d seen the buck the previous day, and I climbed up about 15 feet in a super thick area.”

Through his afternoon and evening hunt Dean heard squirrels chattering and scampering through leaves. He turned when he heard something behind him about 5:30 p.m. and when he looked he spotted the buck standing in a thick growth of mountain laurel.

“All I could see was his rack,” he says. “I pushed the safety off my Ravin crossbow, and when he heard that he looked right at me. He stared at me without moving for about a minute, but it seemed like hours.

“Then he put his head down, took a few steps into an opening. I raised my crossbow, put the scope crosshairs behind his shoulder, and pulled the trigger.”

The buck whirled and took off into a massive thicket of mountain laurel cover. Dean heard the deer plowing through the vegetation, then it became quiet.

“I wasn’t very confident, and when I climbed down with my stand I went to where he stood,” Dean says. “I found just a little blood, but no arrow. I called a few friends, and my dad, and a buddy who knew where I was hunting said he would be there as quickly as possible.”

His friend got there after dark, but with lights they found a slight blood trail leading into very thick cover.

“It was so thick we almost stepped on my deer,” Dean says. “The buck only went about 65 yards from where I’d shot it. But in the laurel cover you can’t see anything.”

His 100-grain Beast expandable two-blade broadhead had been plenty lethal, piercing both lungs and the buck’s heart.

“There was no dragging the deer anywhere in that laurel and steep rocky area,” Dean says. “We dressed and quartered the buck and packed him out like it was a Western hunt for elk.”

Dean estimates his 17-point buck weighed about 180 pounds, live weight. He will have the deer aged by a state official. But he believes it’s perhaps 7.5 years old, and the same one he’d been after for years in that specific mountain area.

“There aren’t that many deer in that region, and even fewer big bucks,” he says. “In my mind, it had to be the same buck I’d chased for four years.”

Read Next: A Veteran Bowhunter Finds Two Bucks Locked Together Deep Into Public Land. Should He Shoot Them?

Dean says when his taxidermist was caping the buck for mounting, he found a wound on its neck. Dean believes that confirms his belief the buck is the same deer he’d chased for so many seasons.

“Last year I was walking in to hunt and I jumped a doe, and behind it at 60 yards was the buck,” Dean says. “I shot at it as it faced me, and I was convinced I missed. But the slight flesh wound on the side of its neck found when it was caped, proves to me it was that same buck. My arrow must have grazed its neck.”

No official measurements of the buck have been made, but eventually Dean will have it scored by Buckmasters.

Bob McNally has been an outdoor writer since shortly after the earth’s crust cooled. He has written 12 outdoor books, more than 5,000 outdoor magazine stories (including many for Outdoor Life) and more newspaper outdoor columns and features than there are hairs on a grizzly bear.

U.S. Airlines Face New Collusion Lawsuits-Sharing Data To Keep Ticket Prices High

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Airfares rose 22.4% over the past year, according to the government’s September inflation report. And American Airlines, Delta, United other major carriers are accused of using shared airfare and customer shopping data to keep ticket prices high. Two new class action lawsuits say the airlines have built a way to spot competitors’ discounts and discourage the price cutting that could save passengers money. There’s a plausible antitrust claim here. There’s also a straightforward explanation for much of the same activity: airlines need to know when they’re charging more than competitors so they can lower their prices. The kind of information sharing that’s happening does not – on its own – amount to a legal violation. I just wrote about former American Airlines CEO Doug Parker explaining how airlines use Wall Street analysts to pressure competitors to stop growing, even while denying that executives signal each other. These lawsuits raise a related question about the systems pricing departments use every day: whether airlines have agreed to share information in a way that helps them avoid competing. Cory Garner flags two new complaints: Ward v. ATPCO, filed in federal court in Seattle, and Brown v. ATPCO, filed in Virginia. Between them, they name American Airlines, Delta Air Lines, United Airlines, Southwest Airlines, Alaska, Hawaiian and JetBlue, along with the Airline Tariff Publishing Company and its 3Victors subsidiary. ATPCO is owned by airlines and has long distributed fares and fare rules. These cases target its newer shopping data business. Airlines increasingly generate offers that vary by channel, itinerary, bundle and customer. Those offers can be harder for competitors to observe than a published fare. Iris collects shopping data. Airlines and other participants contribute information and receive competitive information in return. PriceEye makes offers easier to compare. It supplies competitive data and alerts that draw attention to differences in pricing. DemandView identifies demand before it becomes bookings. Search activity can show where customers want to travel even when they haven’t bought tickets. 3Victors describes the information exchange as “the more you contribute, the more you receive.” And it says Iris collects all the offers returned by a shopping request, including different brands, cabins and prices which is more than an airline would get scraping a limited website search result. The lawsuits argue this gives competitors visibility into competitor pricing they wouldn’t otherwise have, including for offers that haven’t resulted in sales. An airline that might discount to fill seats instead knows rivals can quickly discover and respond to the offer, so they do not make it. If an airline expects every price cut to be matched almost immediately, it may see less opportunity to win business by cutting in the first place. The same information can make tickets cheaper. If an airline is selling a flight for $100 more than a competitor, getting an alert that they’re being underpriced lets them adjust their fares to avoid losing a sale. And better demand information can support an extra flight or a larger aircraft, adding seat supply which supports lower fares. As I’ve explained when American, United and Southwest all showed the same $523 fare, independently responding to a rival’s price isn’t an antitrust violation. Similar prices aren’t enough to establish an agreement. An airline can generally observe a publicly available price and decide to match it. Competitors agreeing to keep prices above a certain level is price fixing. An agreement to exchange sensitive information that makes competition less vigorous is somewhere in beteween – it’s alleged here that the agreement violates the Sherman Act by unreasonably restraining competition. The lawsuit itself doesn’t establish that happens – they need to get to the discovery stage and come up with something juicy. In United States v. Container Corp., the Supreme Court found reciprocal exchange of recent customer-specific prices even without an agreement to adhere to a price schedule to violate the law. On the other hand, the Court also recognized that information exchanges can improve competition. Garner points out that the old government case against ATPCO involved airlines circulating proposed fares before customers could buy them. Airlines could see whether competitors would go along with an increase before exposing themselves to losing sales. PriceEye’s ability to show current offers for future flights isn’t the same thing. A price you can buy today for travel next June isn’t advance notice of a planned price increase. I’m skeptical that what the airlines are doing here actually breaks the law. At a minimum, their defenses are pretty good. A faster, more reliable way to observe bookable fares can help airlines compete. These systems don’t make pricing decisions. There’s no common agent fixing fares. Higher fares alone don’t show this system caused them. Fuel prices and resulting capacity discipline have mattered a lot. Meanwhile, airfares have actually been falling in inflation adjusted terms and even with the recent run-up are lower than they were pre-pandemic. Assuming the cases make it through to discovery, actual contracts airlines signed and feeds will show agreements and the real data being shared, as well as how the services are marketed and discussed at the airlines. If the case goes very far, I’d imagine we’d see a settlement involving money and tighter limits on data sharing to avoid triple damages plus fees. Topics on this pageAntitrust Law-Sherman Act-State Action Immunity - Immunity Denied to State Approved Utility Practice - Cantor u. Detroit Edison Co.ATPCOPrice fixingSherman Antitrust ActAlaskaAmerican AirlinesClass actionDelta Air LinesDoug ParkerJetBlueSeattleSouthwest AirlinesSupreme Court of the United StatesUnited Airlines+10 more More From View from the Wing

Garner points out that the old government case against ATPCO involved airlines circulating proposed fares before customers could buy them. Airlines could see whether competitors would go along with an increase before exposing themselves to losing sales.

PriceEye’s ability to show current offers for future flights isn’t the same thing. A price you can buy today for travel next June isn’t advance notice of a planned price increase.

I’m skeptical that what the airlines are doing here actually breaks the law. At a minimum, their defenses are pretty good.

A faster, more reliable way to observe bookable fares can help airlines compete.

These systems don’t make pricing decisions. There’s no common agent fixing fares.

Higher fares alone don’t show this system caused them. Fuel prices and resulting capacity discipline have mattered a lot. Meanwhile, airfares have actually been falling in inflation adjusted terms and even with the recent run-up are lower than they were pre-pandemic. Assuming the cases make it through to discovery, actual contracts airlines signed and feeds will show agreements and the real data being shared, as well as how the services are marketed and discussed at the airlines. If the case goes very far, I’d imagine we’d see a settlement involving money and tighter limits on data sharing to avoid triple damages plus fees. DOJ just launched something called the Super Intelligence Task Force

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The unit is tasked with ensuring that the technology is not “manipulated by bad actors at home or abroad.”

The Department of Justice (DOJ) just announced the creation of something called the Super Intelligence Task Force, also called the DOJ-SITF. The unit is tasked with making sure that US-based artificial intelligence technology isn't compromised by "foreign state and state-sponsored actors, transnational criminal organizations and individual or corporate actors engaged in fraud."

It's being led by Stanley Woodward and Stephen Weiss, two people who report to Attorney General Todd Blanche. I bring Blanche up because he has been accused of fraud on several occasions, once via a crypto scheme, and now he's involved with an agency that will investigate it in the AI sector.

"SI development will continue to be powered by American innovators," Blanche said in a press release announcing the unit. "The SI Task Force at the Department of Justice will facilitate our partnership with SI Force Chair Jay Clayton as we ensure critical SI technologies serve the American people and are not manipulated by bad actors at home or abroad."

This seemed ridiculous several weeks ago when President Trump first coined the term, being as how the entire world had been calling it artificial intelligence for years and years.

Trump has since said he is banning the term "artificial intelligence" after becoming angry when The Wall Street Journal didn't immediately adopt his preferred nomenclature. Most publications have refused to jump on board here, primarily because it's needless and confusing. The rest of the world calls it AI so why muck things up?

That leads us to our beloved tech CEOs. These people have proven they will happily bow to Trump to protect their business interests. Google has followed the president down the Lake America and Gulf of America rabbit holes. Former Apple CEO Tim Cook gave him a fancy gold statue for no real reason. All of these companies also donated a ton of money to that ballroom we all so desperately want.

But will they continue to do whatever he asks, no matter how ridiculous, after the midterms? Will they use the term SI? That remains to be seen. These poor billionaires and their companies are in something of a pickle right now. They still need Trump to continue racking up massive government contracts.

However, newly-elected Democrat majorities are likely to start investigations into what these companies were up to throughout the past two years. Cozying up to the president right now could be a bad look.

Jordan Poorman Cocker’s Vision of Global Indigeneity

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The curator, who just organized a major rehang of Indigenous art at Crystal Bridges, reflects on the ways institutions can create space for Native artists to flourish.

BENTONVILLE, Ark. — On October 16 and 17, before a room full of Indigenous artists, curators, and scholars at the Crystal Bridges Museum of American Art’s symposium on contemporary Native art, Jordan Poorman Cocker opened with a land acknowledgment. Founded by Walmart heiress Alice Walton, the museum sits on the ancestral homelands of the Osage Nation, Quapaw Nation, and Caddo Nation, who were forcibly relocated in the 19th century. Poorman Cocker, a Kiowa and Tongan citizen, was hired as the museum’s Native American Graves Protection and Repatriation Act (NAGPRA) officer and first full-time curator of Indigenous art in 2023. She makes the hour-and-a-half drive in from Tahlequah, Oklahoma, the Cherokee Nation’s capital and home to several nations coerced there during the removal era.

The two-day gathering coincided with the reinstallation of contemporary Native art in the museum, which Poorman Cocker spearheaded alongside Ashley Holland (Cherokee Nation) as a way to showcase the diversity of style, medium, and living Native artists across Indian Country, including works by citizens of the ancestral Tribes in Arkansas. Choctaw and Cherokee multi-disciplinary artist Jeffrey Gibson delivered a keynote address, and later spoke to an audience with Poorman Cocker while beading; Diné weaver Melissa Cody traced the lineage of her work in dialogue with her mother Lola Cody; and Lehuauakea delivered a workshop based in her traditional kapa practice.

Poorman Cocker’s own curatorial practice draws on her background in museum studies at Victoria University of Wellington in New Zealand and the global spirit of Indigeneity, which rejects the divisive borders of colonial nation-states. Like many Native curators, she cites the groundbreaking 2019 exhibition Hearts of Our People: Native Women Artists at the Minneapolis Institute of Art as a major source of inspiration. That sentiment was echoed by Kiowa beadwork artist Terri Greeves, who spoke at the symposium alongside other Kiowa Tribe members and matriarchs. Poorman Cocker is also a beadwork and textile artist herself, rooted in Kiowa and Tongan beadwork and textile conventions.

In an email correspondence with Hyperallergic, Poorman Cocker shared more about a recent landmark commission, telling a fuller history of Native art, and what it means to bring Kiowa voices into the museum. The interview has been lightly edited and condensed for clarity.

Hyperallergic: Tell me more about what the reinstallation at Crystal Bridges represents for Indigenous art in this moment. What is it trying to change? How did the 114,000-square-foot expansion inform the museum’s capacity to provide ongoing opportunities to collaborate with the featured artists?

JPC: The impetus is to recenter two primary areas: the Indigenous art collection and the craft collection. Native American and Indigenous art are foundational to every American art collection. Over the years, I have written acquisition proposals for artworks sourced from the gallery system and annual markets, with an emphasis on artist commissions.  Crystal Bridges is better positioned to nourish the art ecosystem, including many artists — particularly those whose work aligns with intergenerational art forms, who need financial support for artist apprenticeships, community classes, and other art education efforts that sustain artist communities and knowledge systems.

Indigenous and Native art makes up about 3% of Crystal Bridges’s permanent collection. The museum sector writ large has a long way to go to better present Indigenous artists and stories.

Gnosis Pay Breach Exposes User Data, Triggering Immediate Phishing Alerts

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Disclaimer: The opinions expressed by our writers are their own and do not represent the views of U.Today. The financial and market information provided on U.Today is intended for informational purposes only. U.Today is not liable for any financial losses incurred while trading cryptocurrencies. Conduct your own research by contacting financial experts before making any investment decisions. We believe that all content is accurate as of the date of publication, but certain offers mentioned may no longer be available.

Gnosis Pay, a fintech project from Britain, officially confirmed that data belonging to holders of its retail Visa debit cards was compromised. The breach which took place on November 3 continues the trend of this fall, when attackers no longer need to go after blockchains or smart contracts. It’s far easier for them to hit the traditional fiat infra that links digital assets to the real world.

Institutional partners and investors can exhale as the company’s enterprise and white-label B2B platform is running normally. For ordinary users, the key point is that the crypto in their wallets is safe, as the company said funds were not affected either. Card issuer Monavate Limited, which is regulated by the UK’s Financial Conduct Authority, continues to operate as well. The hackers walked away with data only.

This time the unauthorized access involved a legacy B2C card processing system. The Gnosis Pay platform was already being prepared for retirement, with a full shutdown scheduled for December 20. According to the official explanation, after the hack happened, Gnosis Pay’s security team moved fast to contain the threat and cut off access via the compromised keys within two hours.

The Gnosis Pay incident rounds out a trilogy of major leaks this fall and exposes a weak spot shared by crypto-fiat gateways. The timeline of the past few weeks points to a clear pattern evident in Ledger, Trezor, SafePal and Keystone leaks throughout 2026.

Gnosis Pay has finished emailing affected users with a detailed list of the exposed data. Until the system goes offline next month, the company is operating in emergency mode with transaction monitoring being upped, and 3D Secure verification is now mandatory for all online payments.

Of course, the company has also issued an urgent phishing alert. Armed with purchase details, addresses and personal data, scammers are already starting to pose as official customer support. The Gnosis Pay team urges users to be suspicious of all incoming requests and reminds them that “we and our Partner Apps will never ask” for security credentials such as seed phrases, private keys, PINs or full card numbers to verify or cancel transactions. Finally, any account changes are legitimate only when users make them directly within the official app.
